Write $Q$ for the law of the replicas together with $A$, and use $D,F,J$ for their difference matrix, difference span, and affine volume as in <a href="#eq:equal-label-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equal-label-geometry">[eq:equal-label-geometry]</a>.

<a id="eq:gaussian-correlation-upper"></a>
For large $d$, $k+m\le d-2$. Corollary <a href="#cor:replica-likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:replica-likelihood">16</a> applies with $$c=(2\pi)^{-km/2},\qquad
 R(d\mathbf s,dA)=p^{\otimes t}(d\mathbf s)\gamma_{k,d}^{F}(dA).$$ The evaluated density is the common exact version of Theorem <a href="#thm:equal-label-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:equal-label-measure">15</a>. If $K$ is the total correlation of the replicas, Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a> gives $$\label{eq:gaussian-correlation-upper}
 K\le\log c-k\mathbb E_Q\log J-m\mathbb E_Q\log p_A(Y)<\infty.$$ The potentially large term is the negative logarithm of $J$. We recover it from a test determined by the projected replicas. After that cancellation, only a comparison between two equal-label projection densities remains.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The observable projected test

Let $B$ be the independent $\ell$-row matrix in Theorem <a href="#thm:gaussian-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gaussian-block">1</a>, and set $U_i=BS_i$. The projected tuple determines $$BD=[U_2-U_1,\ldots,U_t-U_1].$$ At its almost sure full rank, choose a $k\times\ell$ matrix $C_0$ with orthonormal rows in the orthogonal complement of the columns of $BD$, measurably as a function of $BD$. Projecting the standard basis and taking the first successful Gram–Schmidt choices supplies such a rule. There is room for these rows because $\ell-m=3k$. Define the selector arbitrarily on rank failures. Put $$\widetilde A=C_0B.$$ This matrix is known from $(B,U_1,\ldots,U_t)$ and satisfies $\widetilde A S_i=C_0U_1$ for every replica. It supplies an observable equal-label likelihood to compare with the actual likelihood in <a href="#eq:gaussian-correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-correlation-upper">[eq:gaussian-correlation-upper]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-gaussian-test"></a>
<div id="lem:gaussian-test" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:gaussian-projected-test"></a>
<a id="eq:gaussian-correlation-loss"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Projected likelihood test). *Let $G$ be an $\ell\times m$ standard Gaussian matrix and set $$Z_{k,m}=\mathbb E\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)^{-k/2}.$$ This constant is finite and positive. The nonnegative function $$\label{eq:gaussian-projected-test}
 \Lambda(B,U_1,\ldots,U_t)
 =\frac{c}{Z_{k,m}}
  \det((BD)^{\mathsf T}(BD))^{-k/2}
  p_{C_0B}(C_0U_1)^{-m},$$ with value zero on the exceptional null sets, has expectation one under the law that draws $B$ and projects independent $p$-distributed signals. Conditional on the replica tuple and its original matrix $A$, $\widetilde A$ has law $\gamma_{k,d}^{F}$; this law depends only on the tuple. If $K'$ is the projected total correlation of the replicas, then $$\label{eq:gaussian-correlation-loss}
 K-K'\le Ckm+
 m\mathbb E\!\left[\log p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1)-\log p_A(Y)\right].$$ Every logarithm in this display is absolutely integrable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-test"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian-test</summary>

*Proof.* For a fixed full-rank tuple write $D=ET$, where the columns of $E$ form an orthonormal basis of $F$ and $T$ is invertible. Then $G=BE$ is standard Gaussian and $$\det((BD)^{\mathsf T}(BD))
 =J^2\det(G^{\mathsf T}G).$$ The action of $B$ on $F^\perp$ is independent of $G$. Conditional on $G$ and the tuple, $C_0$ is fixed with orthonormal rows and $C_0BE=0$. Thus $\widetilde A=C_0B$ has law $\gamma_{k,d}^{F}$, independently of $G$ given the tuple. Since $B$ is independent of the replica experiment, this remains its law after conditioning also on the original $A$. The test is observable from $(B,U_1,\ldots,U_t)$: $C_0$, $C_0B$, and its evaluated label $C_0U_1$ are all determined there. The common density versions of Theorem <a href="#thm:equal-label-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:equal-label-measure">15</a> make the last factor measurable, finite, and positive almost surely under the independent-tuple reference. The replica tuple is absolutely continuous with respect to that reference’s tuple marginal, so the same assertion holds for replicas.

Under the independent-signal reference law, integrate the factor $\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)^{-k/2}$ first. It contributes $Z_{k,m}$. The remaining expectation is $$\int cJ^{-k}p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1)^{-m}
       p^{\otimes t}(d\mathbf s)\gamma_{k,d}^{F}(d\widetilde A)=1$$ by Corollary <a href="#cor:replica-likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:replica-likelihood">16</a>. This proves the normalization of <a href="#eq:gaussian-projected-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-projected-test">[eq:gaussian-projected-test]</a>.

<a id="eq:gaussian-wishart-remainder"></a>
Here are the needed Gaussian determinant bounds. Gram–Schmidt expresses $\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)$ as a product of independent chi-squares with degrees $\nu=\ell,\ell-1,\ldots,\ell-m+1$; this standard Gaussian reduction is also described by Edelman and Rao (Edelman and Rao 2005, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[EdelmanRao2005](#ref-EdelmanRao2005)]. The smallest degree is $3k+1>k$. Since $k$ is even, direct integration of the chi-square density gives $$\mathbb E(\chi_\nu^2)^{-k/2}
 =\prod_{j=1}^{k/2}(\nu-2j)^{-1}\le(2k)^{-k/2}.$$ In particular $Z_{k,m}<\infty$. The same densities give absolute logarithmic integrability, and Jensen gives $\mathbb E\log\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)\le m\log\ell$. Consequently $$\label{eq:gaussian-wishart-remainder}
 \log Z_{k,m}+\frac{k}{2}\mathbb E\log\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)\le Ckm.$$

<a id="eq:gaussian-negative-moment"></a>
Before taking logarithms of the test, we control the positive logarithm of its projection density. For a fixed tuple put $$\delta(s')=\lVert P_{F^\perp}(s'-S_1)\rVert.$$ The ball-average density version in Theorem <a href="#thm:equal-label-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:equal-label-measure">15</a> holds at $\widetilde A S_1$ under the independent-tuple reference and hence under the replica law. The coordinates of $\widetilde A(s'-S_1)$ are independent centered Gaussians with variance $\delta(s')^2$. A ball has probability at most its volume times the maximum Gaussian density. Apply this bound to the ball averages and then use Fatou’s lemma: $$\label{eq:gaussian-negative-moment}
 \mathbb E_{\widetilde A}p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1)
 \le (2\pi)^{-k/2}\int\delta(s')^{-k}\,p(ds').$$ Here and below such conditional estimates hold for almost every tuple. The affine space $S_1+F$ has dimension $m$, so <a href="#eq:affine-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-tube">[eq:affine-tube]</a> gives $$p\{\delta\le r\}\le LC^d r^{d-1-m},\qquad 0<r\le1.$$ Since $d-1-m>k$, tail integration bounds the inverse moment uniformly over tuples for fixed $d,L$. Thus $\log^+p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1)$ is integrable.

<a id="eq:gaussian-test-log"></a>
The projected law has divergence $K'\le K<\infty$ from the independent-signal reference. Normalization of the test and <a href="#eq:entropy-positive-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-positive-log">[eq:entropy-positive-log]</a> imply that $(\log\Lambda)^+$ is integrable. Its defining identity is $$\label{eq:gaussian-test-log}
 \begin{aligned}
 \log\Lambda
 &=\log c-\log Z_{k,m}-k\log J
   -\frac{k}{2}\log\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)\\
 &\qquad-m\log p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1).
 \end{aligned}$$ The negative part of this expression is bounded by a constant and the positive logarithms of $J$, the Gaussian determinant, and the last density. Those are integrable by Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a>, the chi-square calculation, and <a href="#eq:gaussian-negative-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-negative-moment">[eq:gaussian-negative-moment]</a>. Thus $\log\Lambda$ is absolutely integrable. Solving <a href="#eq:gaussian-test-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-test-log">[eq:gaussian-test-log]</a> for the last logarithm proves its absolute integrability as well.

We may now apply the entropy test <a href="#eq:entropy-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-test">[eq:entropy-test]</a> to obtain $K'\ge\mathbb E\log\Lambda$. Subtract this bound from <a href="#eq:gaussian-correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-correlation-upper">[eq:gaussian-correlation-upper]</a>. Equation <a href="#eq:gaussian-test-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-test-log">[eq:gaussian-test-log]</a> displays the exact cancellation of $-k\mathbb E\log J$, and <a href="#eq:gaussian-wishart-remainder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-wishart-remainder">[eq:gaussian-wishart-remainder]</a> bounds the remaining determinant terms by $Ckm$. This gives <a href="#eq:gaussian-correlation-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-correlation-loss">[eq:gaussian-correlation-loss]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Comparing the remaining projection densities

Both the actual $A$ and the observable $\widetilde A$ annihilate $F$. The preceding lemma has removed the affine volume from the information bound. It remains to compare the two densities in <a href="#eq:gaussian-correlation-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-correlation-loss">[eq:gaussian-correlation-loss]</a>. We first bound the conditional mean of the new density by a sum of averages of the actual density. The radii in that sum will depend only on $A$, allowing us to average the actual label with its original density $p_A$.

<a id="stmt-lem-gaussian-density"></a>
<div id="lem:gaussian-density" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Dyadic density comparison). *In the replica experiment with the independent side matrix and $\widetilde A=C_0B$ defined above, <a id="eq:gaussian-density-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:gaussian-density-comparison}
 \mathbb E\!\left[\log p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1)-\log p_A(Y)\right]
 \le Ck+C\log(2+\log L).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian-density</summary>

<a id="eq:gaussian-cutoff"></a>
<a id="eq:gaussian-density-sum"></a>
*Proof.* Fix the tuple and its actual $(A,Y)$, and again write $\delta(s')=\lVert P_{F^\perp}(s'-S_1)\rVert$. Conditional on these data, $\widetilde A$ has law $\gamma_{k,d}^{F}$. We refine the inverse-moment bound <a href="#eq:gaussian-negative-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-negative-moment">[eq:gaussian-negative-moment]</a> by using $A$. Choose the deterministic integer $$\label{eq:gaussian-cutoff}
 J_0=1+\left\lceil
       \frac{\log_2 L+C_1d}{d-1-m-k}\right\rceil$$ with a sufficiently large absolute $C_1$. The denominator is bounded below by a positive absolute multiple of $d$. The contribution of $\delta\le2^{-J_0}$ to the inverse moment, including the Gaussian constant, is at most $$(2\pi)^{-k/2}
 \sum_{j\ge J_0}2^{(j+1)k}LC^d2^{-j(d-1-m)}\le1.$$ For the remaining distances use the bins $\delta\in(r/2,r]$, where $r=2,1,\ldots,2^{-J_0+1}$. These cover the remaining values because $\delta(s')\le\lVert s'-S_1\rVert\le2$. Since $A$ annihilates $F$, $$\lVert As'-Y\rVert\le\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\,\delta(s').$$ Let $v_k$ be the unit $k$-ball volume, and let $\overline p_A^{\,a}(y)$ be the average of $p_A$ over the radius-$a$ ball centered at $y$. The contribution of one bin is at most $$(2/r)^k p\{\delta\le r\}
 \le 2^k v_k\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{k}
       \overline p_A^{\,\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}r}(Y).$$ Integrating $e^{-\lVert z\rVert^2/2}$ on the radius-$\sqrt{k}$ ball gives $v_k\le(2\pi e/k)^{k/2}$. Hence $$\label{eq:gaussian-density-sum}
 \mathbb E_{\widetilde A}p_{\widetilde A}(\widetilde A S_1)
 \le1+\left(\frac{C\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)^k
       \sum_r\overline p_A^{\,\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}r}(Y).$$ The right side depends on the tuple only through $(A,Y)$.

<a id="eq:gaussian-density-jensen"></a>
Divide by the finite positive density $p_A(Y)$ and apply Jensen first to $\widetilde A$; all logarithms are integrable by Lemma <a href="#lem:gaussian-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-test">19</a>. Only after obtaining <a href="#eq:gaussian-density-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-density-sum">[eq:gaussian-density-sum]</a> do we average over $Y$ given $A$. Its law is then the original $p_A(y)\,dy$, and for every radius fixed by $A$, $$\mathbb E_{Y\mid A}\frac{\overline p_A^{\,a}(Y)}{p_A(Y)}\le1,\qquad
 \mathbb E_{Y\mid A}\frac1{p_A(Y)}
 \le v_k\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^{k}.$$ The first inequality integrates an averaged probability density over $\{p_A>0\}$, and the second is the volume of that set, contained in the radius-$\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ ball. A second application of Jensen thus bounds the expected log ratio by $$\label{eq:gaussian-density-jensen}
 k\mathbb E\log\!\left(C\left(1+\frac{\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt{k}}\right)\right)
       +\log(J_0+2).$$ This order of averaging is essential: before the tuple has disappeared, its conditional label need not have density $p_A$ under further conditioning.

For completeness, choose $1/4$-nets of the unit spheres in $\mathbb R^d$ and $\mathbb R^k$, with at most $9^d$ and $9^k$ points. The operator norm is at most twice the maximum absolute Gaussian bilinear form on these nets. The expected maximum of $N$ absolute standard normals is at most $\sqrt{2\log(2N)}$, by the exponential-moment bound; their independence is not required. Thus $\mathbb E\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le C\sqrt d$. Since $k$ is a fixed positive fraction of $d$, Jensen bounds the first term of <a href="#eq:gaussian-density-jensen" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-density-jensen">[eq:gaussian-density-jensen]</a> by $Ck$. The cutoff <a href="#eq:gaussian-cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-cutoff">[eq:gaussian-cutoff]</a> gives $\log(J_0+2)\le C\log(2+\log L)$, proving the lemma. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-gaussian-density-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-gaussian-density</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:gaussian-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gaussian-block">1</a>.* Generate $W$ conditionally on $(A,Y)$ independently of the replicas. Each pair $(S_i,W)$ has the original pair law, and the side matrix $B$ is independent of the whole construction. Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-information">13</a>, followed by Lemmas <a href="#lem:gaussian-test" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-test">19</a> and <a href="#lem:gaussian-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:gaussian-density">20</a>, gives $$tI(S;W\mid B,BS)
 \le H(W)+Ckm+m\bigl(Ck+C\log(2+\log L)\bigr).$$ Here the representative signal is coupled independently with $B$, exactly as in the original block experiment. Since $t=m+1$ and $k$ is proportional to $d$, division by $t$ proves <a href="#eq:gaussian-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-block">[eq:gaussian-block]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:distance-bins"></a>
### Shared data and distance-bin comparisons

The determinant test in the preceding section pays a double logarithm of the largest prior density. We instead encode the dyadic scale of each projected simplex height. Under a prior bounded by $e^b$, these scales have entropy $O(1+b/d)$. The projected correlation recovers the height and label-density terms, leaving this scale cost. Since the chosen scale depends on the side matrix, the central step compares it with a mixture over fixed scales before averaging the matrix.

Throughout this section, for sufficiently large $d$, set <a id="eq:distance-dimensions"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-dimensions}
 k=\lfloor d/16\rfloor,\qquad q=\lfloor d/8\rfloor,\qquad
 p=\lfloor d/2\rfloor.$$ Here $k$ is the number of actual rows, $q$ is the number of replicas, and $p$ is the number of side rows. In particular $k\ge d/32$, $q\ge d/16$, $q\ge2$, and $p-i+2\ge2k$ for $2\le i\le q$.

<a id="stmt-thm-distance-block"></a>
<div id="thm:distance-block" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:distance-block"></a>
**Theorem 21** (Information from shared exact data). *Let $P=f\sigma_d$ be a probability with a nonnegative Borel density $f\le e^b$, where $b\ge0$. Let $S\sim P$, let $A$ be an independent $k\times d$ standard Gaussian matrix, and put $Y=AS$. Let a finite message $W$ be drawn from a kernel of $(A,Y)$, conditionally independently of $S$. Let $B$ be a $p\times d$ standard Gaussian matrix independent of the entire joint experiment producing $(S,A,Y,W)$. Then $$\label{eq:distance-block}
 I(S;W\mid B,BS)\le\frac{H(W)}q+C\left(d+\frac bd\right)$$ for an absolute $C$, uniformly over the prior and message kernel.*

</div>

The order of <a href="#eq:distance-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-block">[eq:distance-block]</a> also follows from Theorem <a href="#thm:gaussian-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gaussian-block">1</a>: append $k$ ignored fresh rows to $A$ and first use the initial $8k$ rows of $B$. The Gaussian theorem gives the entropy denominator $2k+1\ge q$, and $8k\le p$. Adding the remaining independent side rows can only decrease the conditional information, because independence of the side matrix gives $$I(S;W\mid B,BS)=I(S;W)-I(W;B,BS).$$ Finally $\log(2+b)\le\log(2+d)+b/d\le Cd+b/d$. The direct proof below identifies the entropy of the chosen distance scales as the prior cost.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The shared-data law and its affine heights

<a id="eq:distance-posterior-budget"></a>
Write $g_A^0$ and $g_A$ for the projected densities of $\sigma_d$ and $P$, using the versions in <a href="#eq:finite-tilt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-tilt">[eq:finite-tilt]</a>. Put $\overline f(A,y)=g_A(y)/g_A^0(y)$ on the open ellipsoid. At labels with $g_A(y)>0$, the posterior density on the corresponding uniform fiber is bounded by $\exp(b_{A,y})$, where $$\label{eq:distance-posterior-budget}
 b_{A,y}=b-\log\overline f(A,y)\ge0,\qquad
 \mathbb Eb_{A,Y}=b-\mathbb E_AD_{\mathrm{KL}}(g_A\Vert g_A^0)\le b.$$ These quantities are integrable: the positive density-ratio logarithm is bounded by $b$, and its negative part is controlled by $-z\log z\le1/e$ against the probability density $g_A^0$.

Given $(A,Y)$, draw $S_1,\ldots,S_q$ independently from the posterior. Under this shared-data law, $AS_i=Y$, and each pair $(S_i,A)$ has the original independent signal–matrix law. Define $$F_i=\mathop{\mathrm{aff}}(S_1,\ldots,S_{i-1}),\qquad
 \rho_i=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(S_i,F_i),\qquad 2\le i\le q.$$ The next estimate is stronger than merely proving logarithmic integrability: its prior cost is divided by the ambient dimension.

<a id="stmt-lem-distance-heights"></a>
<div id="lem:distance-heights" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Logarithmic cost of a height). *The heights are positive almost surely and obey <a id="eq:distance-height"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-height}
 \rho_i\le2,\qquad
 \mathbb E\log^-\rho_i\le C\left(1+\frac bd\right),
 \qquad \log^-x:=\max\{0,-\log x\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distance-heights"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distance-heights</summary>

<a id="eq:distance-linear-log"></a>
*Proof.* We first record a uniform-sphere distance estimate. Let $U$ be uniform on $S^{n-1}$ with $n\le d$, and let $E$ be a linear subspace of dimension $j$ whose codimension $h=n-j$ is at least $d/2$. Write a normalized Gaussian representation with independent components $X\in\mathbb R^h$ and $Z\in\mathbb R^j$. For $t\ge\log2$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(U,E)<e^{-t}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \lVert X\rVert\le2e^{-t}\lVert Z\rVert.$$ The Gaussian density bound and the unit-ball volume estimate give $\mathbb P\{\lVert X\rVert\le a\}\le(Ca/\sqrt h)^h$. Also $$\mathbb Ee^{\lVert Z\rVert^2/4}=2^{j/2},\qquad
 \lVert z\rVert^h\le(2h)^{h/2}e^{\lVert z\rVert^2/4}.$$ Since $j\le d\le2h$, these inequalities yield $\mathbb E\lVert Z\rVert^h\le(C\sqrt h)^h$. Hence $$\mathbb P\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(U,E)<e^{-t}\}\le(Ce^{-t})^h.$$ If the law of $U$ is reweighted by a density at most $e^a$, the right side is multiplied by $e^a$ and capped by one. Integrating the logarithmic tail gives $$\label{eq:distance-linear-log}
 \mathbb E\log^-\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(U,E)\le C+\frac ah.$$ The case $j=0$ is immediate and also satisfies this bound.

Condition now on $(A,Y,S_1,\ldots,S_{i-1})$. Let $r$ and $s_0$ be the radius and center of the posterior fiber, and write $u_j=(S_j-s_0)/r$. The vectors lie on the unit sphere in $\ker A$, of ambient dimension $n=d-k$, and the next vector has density at most $e^{b_{A,Y}}$ there. Moreover $$\frac{\rho_i}{r}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u_i,\mathop{\mathrm{aff}}(u_1,\ldots,u_{i-1}))
 \ge\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(u_i,\operatorname{span}(u_1,\ldots,u_{i-1})).$$ The latter span has dimension at most $i-1$ and codimension at least $d-k-q+1\ge d/2$. Apply <a href="#eq:distance-linear-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-linear-log">[eq:distance-linear-log]</a> and then <a href="#eq:distance-posterior-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-posterior-budget">[eq:distance-posterior-budget]</a>. The additional contribution $\mathbb E[-\log r]$ is bounded by an absolute constant: for each fixed signal, rotational invariance of the independent row space of $A$ makes $r$ a uniform-vector distance to a $k$-dimensional linear space, so <a href="#eq:distance-linear-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-linear-log">[eq:distance-linear-log]</a> applies with $a=0$. This proves the negative-log bound. A zero height would make that expectation infinite, so heights are positive almost surely. The bound $\rho_i\le2$ follows because all signals are on the unit sphere. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:distance-likelihood"></a>
Set $$h_k=\mathbb E[-\log g_A(Y)].$$ This is finite by Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a>. Apply Corollary <a href="#cor:replica-likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:replica-likelihood">16</a> with its row count equal to the present $k$, its difference count equal to $q-1$, and its replica count equal to $q$. The required relation $k+q-1\le3d/16-1\le d-2$ holds in the present dimensions. The exact likelihood is $$\label{eq:distance-likelihood}
 \frac{dQ}{d\bigl(P^{\otimes q}(d\mathbf s)\gamma_{k,d}^{F}(dA)\bigr)}
 =(2\pi)^{-k(q-1)/2}J(\mathbf s)^{-k}g_A(As_1)^{1-q}.$$ Here $Q$ is the shared-data law and $F$ is the span of all difference columns. The evaluated density uses the exact versions established in Theorem <a href="#thm:equal-label-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:equal-label-measure">15</a>.

<a id="eq:distance-correlation-upper"></a>
Since $J=\prod_{i=2}^q\rho_i$, all logarithms in <a href="#eq:distance-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-likelihood">[eq:distance-likelihood]</a> are integrable by Lemma <a href="#lem:distance-heights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distance-heights">22</a> and Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a>. Data processing after forgetting $A$ gives the finite upper bound $$\label{eq:distance-correlation-upper}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tc}}(S_1,\ldots,S_q)
 \le(q-1)h_k-k\sum_{i=2}^q\mathbb E\log\rho_i
             -\frac{k(q-1)}2\log(2\pi).$$

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Projected height bins

<a id="eq:distance-projected-height"></a>
Draw the independent side matrix $B$ and put $C_i=BS_i$. Conditional on the tuple, the image of the direction space of $F_i$ has rank $i-2$ almost surely. The orthogonal residual of $S_i$ from $F_i$ has length $\rho_i$. Its image under $B$ is an $N(0,\rho_i^2 I_p)$ vector, independent of the restriction of $B$ to that direction space. Projecting off the image of the direction space shows that $$\label{eq:distance-projected-height}
 T_i:=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(C_i,BF_i)\ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}=\ \rho_i\chi_{m_i},
 \qquad m_i=p-i+2,$$ conditional on the tuple. Here $\chi_{m_i}$ is a chi variable. In particular $m_i\ge2k$.

<a id="eq:distance-bin-budget"></a>
Define the integer bin $$K_i=\left\lceil\log_2(T_i/\sqrt{m_i})\right\rceil,$$ with any fixed value at $T_i=0$, a null event. Use the reference probabilities $\pi_j=2^{-|j|}/3$ for $j\in\mathbb Z$; they sum to one. The chi-square second moment and the Gaussian small-ball bound show uniformly in $m\ge1$ that $$\mathbb E\log(\chi_m/\sqrt m)\le0,\qquad
 \mathbb E|\log(\chi_m/\sqrt m)|\le C.$$ For the first inequality use Jensen on the square. For the second, its positive part follows from the second moment, while the negative part follows by integrating $\mathbb P\{\chi_m/\sqrt m<e^{-t}\}\le\min\{1,(Ce^{-t})^m\}$. Together with Lemma <a href="#lem:distance-heights" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:distance-heights">22</a>, this gives $$\label{eq:distance-bin-budget}
 (\log2)\mathbb EK_i\le\mathbb E\log\rho_i+\log2,\qquad
 H(K_i)\le\mathbb E[-\log\pi_{K_i}]
       \le C\left(1+\frac bd\right).$$ The entropy bound is the elementary cross-entropy inequality, since $-\log\pi_j=\log3+|j|\log2$ and the expected absolute bin index is bounded.

<a id="eq:distance-bin-information"></a>
For fixed $B$, let $P_B$ be the law of $BS'$ for $S'\sim P$. Given $(B,C_1,\ldots,C_{i-1})$, the affine set $BF_i$ is known. Let $E_j$ be its tube of radius $\sqrt{m_i}\,2^j$. The bin event $K_i=j$ is contained in $E_j$. Since the marginal of $C_i$ given $B$ is $P_B$, conditional relative entropy followed by the bin map yields $$\label{eq:distance-bin-information}
 I(C_i;C_1,\ldots,C_{i-1}\mid B)
 \ge-H(K_i\mid B,C_1,\ldots,C_{i-1})-\mathbb E\log P_B(E_{K_i}).$$ To justify the logarithms, coordinatewise data processing makes the projected total correlation finite by <a href="#eq:distance-correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-correlation-upper">[eq:distance-correlation-upper]</a>. Each conditional divergence in its chain rule is therefore finite. Its image under the bin map is finite as well. The expected negative logarithm of the reference bin probability is that image divergence plus the finite conditional bin entropy. Since the tube probability is at least the bin probability, $-\log P_B(E_{K_i})$ is integrable. This also proves <a href="#eq:distance-bin-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-bin-information">[eq:distance-bin-information]</a> without an undefined entropy difference.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A fixed mixture before averaging the side matrix

<a id="eq:distance-tube-goal"></a>
We will prove the one-height estimate $$\label{eq:distance-tube-goal}
 \mathbb E\log P_B(E_{K_i})
 \le k\mathbb E\log\rho_i-h_k+C\left(k+1+\frac bd\right).$$ Together with <a href="#eq:distance-bin-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-bin-information">[eq:distance-bin-information]</a>, this recovers the terms $h_k-k\mathbb E\log\rho_i$ in the unprojected correlation bound. The selected bin $K_i$ depends on $B$. We first bound every fixed bin using a reference density that depends only on $A$, so that the eventual label average uses the original density $g_A$.

<a id="eq:distance-tube-kernel"></a>
Fix the shared-data tuple and its $(A,Y)$, and fix $i$. For a candidate $s'$, put $\rho'=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(s',F_i)$. Its distance to $BF_i$ after projection has law $\rho'\chi_{m_i}$, by the same calculation as <a href="#eq:distance-projected-height" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-projected-height">[eq:distance-projected-height]</a>. The Gaussian small-ball bound gives $$\mathbb E_B P_B(E_j)
 \le\int\min\left\{1,\left(\frac{4\cdot2^j}{\rho'}\right)^{m_i}\right\}P(ds').$$ The minimum is one at zero denominator. Since $A$ is constant with value $Y$ on $F_i$, $\lVert As'-Y\rVert\le\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\rho'$. Pushing $P$ forward by $A$ therefore gives the further bound $$\label{eq:distance-tube-kernel}
 \mathbb E_B P_B(E_j)
 \le\int\min\left\{1,
       \left(\frac{4\cdot2^j\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{\lVert y'-Y\rVert}\right)^{m_i}\right\}
       g_A(y')\,dy'.$$

<a id="eq:distance-fixed-bin"></a>
For $m>k$ and $\beta>0$, radial integration in $\mathbb R^k$ gives the exact kernel mass <a id="eq:distance-kernel-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-kernel-mass}
 \int_{\mathbb R^k}\min\{1,(\beta/\lVert u\rVert)^m\}\,du
 =v_k\beta^k\frac{m}{m-k}.$$ Indeed the inside ball contributes $v_k\beta^k$, and the exterior contributes $kv_k\beta^k/(m-k)$. Let $a_{A,j}$ be the probability density obtained by convolving $g_A$ with this normalized kernel, using $m=m_i$ and $\beta=4\cdot2^j\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$. Since $m_i\ge2k$ and $v_k\le(2\pi e/k)^{k/2}$, <a href="#eq:distance-tube-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-tube-kernel">[eq:distance-tube-kernel]</a> becomes $$\label{eq:distance-fixed-bin}
 2^{-kj}\mathbb E_B P_B(E_j)
 \le\left(\frac{C\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt k}\right)^k a_{A,j}(Y).$$ For each fixed $i$, this density depends on $A$ and $j$, but not on the replica tuple and not on $Y$ as a parameter of the density.

<a id="eq:distance-mixture"></a>
Define $$a_A=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}\pi_j a_{A,j},\qquad
 T_B=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}\pi_j2^{-kj}P_B(E_j).$$ The first is a probability density depending only on $A$ and the fixed index $i$. Conditional on the shared-data outcome, <a href="#eq:distance-fixed-bin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-fixed-bin">[eq:distance-fixed-bin]</a> gives $$\mathbb E_B T_B\le
 \left(\frac{C\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt k}\right)^k a_A(Y).$$ The $j=K_i$ summand gives the pointwise inequality [(eq:distance-mixture)](#eq:distance-mixture)$$\label{eq:distance-mixture}
 \log P_B(E_{K_i})
 \le kK_i\log2-\log\pi_{K_i}
   +\log T_B.$$

Before averaging logarithms, we verify their integrability. If $Z_{A,j}=v_k(4\cdot2^j\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}})^k m_i/(m_i-k)$, then for $y$ in the support of $g_A$, $$\frac{\pi_0}{Z_{A,0}}\le a_A(y)\le\sup g_A.$$ The lower bound holds because the support has diameter at most $2\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$, so the unnormalized kernel at $j=0$ equals one throughout that support. The upper bound follows by convolving a bounded density with a probability density. The positive log of $\sup g_A$ is integrable by the uniform projected-density formula and the Gaussian log-determinant bound. The absolute log of $Z_{A,0}$ is integrable: $\log^+\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ is controlled by its finite mean, and $\log^-\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le\log^-\lVert Au_0\rVert$ for any fixed unit $u_0$, whose norm is chi-distributed. Thus $\log a_A(Y)$ is absolutely integrable.

The pointwise inequality <a href="#eq:distance-mixture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-mixture">[eq:distance-mixture]</a> bounds $\log^-T_B$ by the sum of $-\log P_B(E_{K_i})$, $k|K_i|\log2$, and $-\log\pi_{K_i}$, all integrable. For the positive part, $\mathbb E_B\log^+T_B\le\log(1+\mathbb E_BT_B)$, and <a href="#eq:distance-fixed-bin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-fixed-bin">[eq:distance-fixed-bin]</a> bounds its right side using $a_A(Y)$ and $\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ as above. Thus $\log T_B$ is absolutely integrable as well.

Conditional Jensen now gives $$\mathbb E_B\log T_B\le
 k\log\left(\frac{C\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}}{\sqrt k}\right)+\log a_A(Y).$$ The right side depends only on $(A,Y)$. In the full experiment $Y\mid A$ has density $g_A$, and nonnegativity of $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(g_A\Vert a_A)$ therefore gives <a id="eq:distance-reference-density"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-reference-density}
 \mathbb E\log a_A(Y)\le\mathbb E\log g_A(Y)=-h_k.$$ This is where the fact that $a_A$ depends only on $A$ is needed. Also $\mathbb E\log(C\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}/\sqrt k)\le C$ by the Gaussian norm estimate in Section <a href="#sec:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gaussian">4</a>, since $k\ge d/32$. Consequently $\mathbb E\log T_B\le Ck-h_k$. Combining this with <a href="#eq:distance-mixture" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-mixture">[eq:distance-mixture]</a> and <a href="#eq:distance-bin-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-bin-budget">[eq:distance-bin-budget]</a> proves <a href="#eq:distance-tube-goal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-tube-goal">[eq:distance-tube-goal]</a>.

<a id="eq:distance-correlation-lower"></a>
Insert <a href="#eq:distance-tube-goal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-tube-goal">[eq:distance-tube-goal]</a> in <a href="#eq:distance-bin-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-bin-information">[eq:distance-bin-information]</a>, use the entropy bound in <a href="#eq:distance-bin-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-bin-budget">[eq:distance-bin-budget]</a>, and sum the conditional divergences over $i=2,\ldots,q$. The projected correlation satisfies $$\label{eq:distance-correlation-lower}
 \mathop{\mathrm{tc}}(C_1,\ldots,C_q\mid B)
 \ge(q-1)h_k-k\sum_{i=2}^q\mathbb E\log\rho_i
          -C(q-1)\left(k+1+\frac bd\right).$$

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-distance-heights-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-distance-heights</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:distance-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distance-block">21</a>.* Generate $W$ from the shared data independently of the replicas conditional on $(A,Y)$. Each replica has the original signal–message pair law, and $B$ is independent of this coupling. The finite correlation bound <a href="#eq:distance-correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-correlation-upper">[eq:distance-correlation-upper]</a> therefore permits Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-information">13</a> with $t=q$: $$qI(S;W\mid B,BS)
 \le H(W)+\mathop{\mathrm{tc}}(S_1,\ldots,S_q)-\mathop{\mathrm{tc}}(C_1,\ldots,C_q\mid B).$$ Subtract <a href="#eq:distance-correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-correlation-lower">[eq:distance-correlation-lower]</a> from <a href="#eq:distance-correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-correlation-upper">[eq:distance-correlation-upper]</a>. The measurement entropy and all height logarithms cancel. Since $k\le d/16$, division by $q$ gives <a href="#eq:distance-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-block">[eq:distance-block]</a>. Section <a href="#sec:streaming-consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:streaming-consequences">6</a> applies this bound while conditioning only on the current state and retaining its separate late elimination of the clock cost. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:streaming-consequences"></a>
### Current-state recurrences and the streaming bound

We now complete the streaming applications of the Gaussian determinant test and the distance-bin test through two current-state iterations. Both use one independent side matrix throughout the stream. They condition on the present padded state, apply a complete bounded-prior block bound in that conditional experiment, and only then average over state values. Thus their information upper bounds never subtract two possibly infinite averages of conditional total correlations.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The Gaussian replica clock

<a id="stmt-prop-gaussian-streaming"></a>
<div id="prop:gaussian-streaming" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23** (Current-state iteration of Gaussian replicas). *Let $M(d)=o(d^2)$ be nonnegative and integer-valued, and let $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$. A learner in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a> whose uniform-prior angular success is at least $3/5$ satisfies $$T(d)\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon(d))$$ for an absolute $c>0$ and all sufficiently large $d$. The threshold may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-gaussian-streaming"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-gaussian-streaming</summary>

*Proof.* Use the first step of Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> to choose a fixed shared-seed conditional experiment with success at least $3/5$ and then take its Borel replacement. Its initialization is independent of the signal and all rows; fresh transition and output randomness remains in the kernels. This selection uses the original jointly measurable experiment and does not require a jointly measurable family of Borel replacements.

Put $\beta=\log(1/\epsilon)$. If $T>d\beta$, the conclusion is immediate. On the remaining branch, Lemmas <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> and <a href="#lem:early-clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:early-clock">11</a> give padded states of entropy at most <a id="eq:gaussian-clock-width"></a>$$\label{eq:gaussian-clock-width}
 h_T=M\log2+\log(T+2)=o(d^2),
 \qquad \beta=o(d),\qquad T=o(d^2).$$ Let $$k=2\lfloor d/16\rfloor,\qquad t=k+1,\qquad
 \ell=4k,\qquad n=\lceil T/k\rceil.$$ Append ignored fresh samples so that the padded stream has $n$ complete blocks. Let $V_i$ be the padded state at the end of block $i$, with $V_0$ the data-independent initial padded state. The latter includes a possible stopping decision at index zero. Draw a single standard Gaussian $\ell\times d$ matrix $B$ independently of the entire selected learner experiment and all appended rows, and put $Z=BS$.

Consider a positive-probability value $V_i=v$, and put $p_v=\mathbb P\{V_i=v\}$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-priors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-priors">10</a>, its conditional signal law has a Borel density $f_v$ satisfying $$f_v\le L_v:=1/p_v
 \quad\hbox{relative to }\sigma_d,\qquad
 \sum_v p_v\log L_v=H(V_i).$$ The next block matrix is independent of the pair $(S,V_i)$: the state uses only the signal, completed rows, and learner randomness, and the new rows are fresh. Given $v$, the next state is a finite kernel of the new matrix $A$ and its exact label $AS$. The side matrix $B$ remains independent of this entire conditional signal, block, and message experiment. Therefore Theorem <a href="#thm:gaussian-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:gaussian-block">1</a> applies with $L=L_v$ and with message $V_{i+1}$.

<a id="eq:gaussian-current-step"></a>
Apply that numerical bound at each $v$ before averaging. Concavity of $x\mapsto\log(2+x)$ and the entropy bound give $$\begin{aligned}
 I(S;V_{i+1}\mid V_i,B,Z)
 &\le \frac{H(V_{i+1}\mid V_i)}{t}
      +Cd+C\sum_vp_v\log(2+\log L_v) \notag\\
 &\le \frac{h_T}{t}+Cd+C\log(2+H(V_i)) \notag\\
 &\le \frac{h_T}{t}+Cd+C\log(2+h_T).
 \label{eq:gaussian-current-step}
\end{aligned}$$ All conditional uses have bounded priors and finite conditional replica correlation, as established in the block theorem. Since $t\asymp d$ and $h_T=o(d^2)$, the last line is at most $C_1d$ for all sufficiently large $d$, with one absolute $C_1$.

<a id="eq:gaussian-information-clock"></a>
Write $\mathcal I(V)=I(S;V\mid B,Z)$. The chain rule and data processing give $$\mathcal I(V_{i+1})
 \le \mathcal I(V_i)+I(S;V_{i+1}\mid V_i,B,Z).$$ Here $\mathcal I(V_0)=0$ by the independence of initialization and the index-zero decision from $(S,B,Z)$. Iterating <a href="#eq:gaussian-current-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-current-step">[eq:gaussian-current-step]</a> yields $$\label{eq:gaussian-information-clock}
 \mathcal I(V_n)\le C_1dn.$$

<a id="eq:gaussian-streaming-endpoint"></a>
The side row count satisfies $\ell=8\lfloor d/16\rfloor\le d/2$. The postponed output is a kernel of $V_n$ with fresh randomness, so <a href="#eq:endpoint-gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-gaussian">[eq:endpoint-gaussian]</a> gives $$\label{eq:gaussian-streaming-endpoint}
 \mathcal I(V_n)\ge
 \tfrac12(d-\ell-1)\log\frac1{5\epsilon}-\log2
 \ge c_1d\beta$$ for large $d$. Lemma <a href="#lem:raw-sample-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:raw-sample-bound">12</a> also gives $T>\lfloor d/2\rfloor$. In particular $T>0$, and for $d\ge32$ the inequality $k\ge d/16$ gives the explicit clock comparison $$dn\le d(T/k+1)\le16T+d\le18T.$$ Combining this with <a href="#eq:gaussian-information-clock" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-information-clock">[eq:gaussian-information-clock]</a> and <a href="#eq:gaussian-streaming-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gaussian-streaming-endpoint">[eq:gaussian-streaming-endpoint]</a> proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### The distance-bin clock

<a id="stmt-prop-distance-streaming"></a>
<div id="prop:distance-streaming" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24** (Current-state iteration of distance bins). *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:streaming">2</a>, the distance-bin block estimate <a href="#eq:distance-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-block">[eq:distance-block]</a> also gives $T(d)\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon(d))$ for an absolute $c>0$ and all sufficiently large $d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-distance-streaming"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-distance-streaming</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> with $p=2/3$ and $p'=3/5$. It first fixes a shared seed using the original jointly measurable experiment, then chooses Borel versions in that conditional experiment and fixes its independent initialization and remaining fresh randomness. We obtain deterministic Borel state-based rules and an output with uniform-prior success at least $3/5$. Pad stopping and postpone this output. The final output is a function of the final tagged state.

For this route keep the convenient clock bound <a id="eq:distance-clock-width"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-clock-width}
 \mathfrak m_T=M\log2+\log(T+3).$$ Indeed, the exact count in Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> is $(T+2)2^M$, so the entropy of every padded state is at most $h_T\le\mathfrak m_T$. Use $$k=\lfloor d/16\rfloor,\qquad q=\lfloor d/8\rfloor,\qquad
 p=\lfloor d/2\rfloor,\qquad n=\lceil T/k\rceil,$$ and append ignored fresh rows through the last block. Let $V_0,\ldots,V_n$ be the block-end states, starting with the data-independent tagged initial state. Independently of the entire deterministic learner experiment and all rows, draw one $p\times d$ standard Gaussian matrix $B$. It is used only for analysis. Put $Z=BS$ and $\mathcal D(V)=I(S;V\mid B,Z)$.

<a id="eq:distance-information-clock"></a>
For a block from $V$ to $W$, condition on a value $v$ with probability $p_v>0$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-priors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-priors">10</a>, the conditional signal density is bounded by $\exp(b_v)$, where $b_v=\log(1/p_v)$. The next standard Gaussian matrix $A$ is independent of $(S,V)$, the ending state is a function of $(v,A,AS)$, and $B$ remains independent of the entire conditional experiment. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:distance-block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:distance-block">21</a> at this fixed $v$ and then average. Since $\sum_vp_vb_v=H(V)$, <a id="eq:distance-current-step"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 I(S;W\mid V,B,Z)
 &\le \frac{H(W\mid V)}q+C\left(d+\frac{H(V)}d\right) \notag\\
 &\le C_2\left(d+\frac{\mathfrak m_T}{d}\right).
 \label{eq:distance-current-step}
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality uses $q\ge d/16$ for large $d$. In this calculation the prior cost $b_v/d$ is averaged linearly; no entire state history is retained. The chain rule gives $\mathcal D(W)-\mathcal D(V)\le I(S;W\mid V,B,Z)$, and $\mathcal D(V_0)=0$. Consequently $$\label{eq:distance-information-clock}
 \mathcal D(V_n)\le C_2 n\left(d+\frac{\mathfrak m_T}{d}\right).$$

<a id="eq:distance-streaming-endpoint"></a>
For the exact side row count $p=\lfloor d/2\rfloor$, the independent residual-sphere endpoint <a href="#eq:endpoint-bins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-bins">[eq:endpoint-bins]</a> gives $$\label{eq:distance-streaming-endpoint}
 \mathcal D(V_n)\ge
 \tfrac12(d-p-1)\log\frac1{4\epsilon}-\log2
 \ge c_2d\beta,\qquad \beta=\log(1/\epsilon),$$ for sufficiently large $d$. This is the direct $90/d$ radius estimate and the event of mass at least $1/2$ used in the distance-bin route. The corresponding raw-data bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:raw-sample-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:raw-sample-bound">12</a> also yields $T>\lfloor d/2\rfloor$, equivalently $T>d/2$ for integer $T$. The zero-row experiment in that lemma has residual radius one.

If $T\ge d\beta$, the asserted lower bound is immediate. Otherwise $\mathcal D(V_n)\le H(V_n)\le\mathfrak m_T$ and <a href="#eq:distance-streaming-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-streaming-endpoint">[eq:distance-streaming-endpoint]</a> give the route’s clock inequality $$c_2d\beta\le M\log2+\log(T+3)
 \le M\log2+\log(d\beta+3).$$ Since $d\beta+3\le(d+3)(\beta+1)$ and $\log(1+\beta)\le\beta$, this implies, for large $d$, <a id="eq:distance-short-clock"></a>$$\label{eq:distance-short-clock}
 \beta\le \frac{C(M+\log d+1)}d=o(d).$$ Thus $T<d\beta=o(d^2)$ and $\mathfrak m_T=o(d^2)$. The right side of <a href="#eq:distance-information-clock" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-information-clock">[eq:distance-information-clock]</a> is consequently at most $C_3nd$. For $d\ge32$, $k\ge d/32$, and $T>d/2$ gives $$dn\le32T+d\le34T.$$ Comparing the resulting upper bound $\mathcal D(V_n)\le C_4T$ with <a href="#eq:distance-streaming-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:distance-streaming-endpoint">[eq:distance-streaming-endpoint]</a> proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-distance-streaming-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-distance-streaming</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:streaming">2</a>.* Its uniform-prior success $2/3$ is at least the $3/5$ required by Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-streaming">23</a>, which proves the theorem. Proposition <a href="#prop:distance-streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:distance-streaming">24</a> gives a second proof using the linear entropy cost. The later sections give three further proofs: the Haar current-state argument in Proposition <a href="#haar:streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:streaming">31</a>, and the synthetic and incidence full-history arguments in Proposition <a href="#syn:streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:streaming">35</a> and Theorem <a href="#inc:precision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:precision">44</a>. Each uses the same learner model, while retaining its own side law, conditional experiment, and clock estimate. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:haar"></a>
### Haar projections and posterior replica information

This comparison uses Haar frames rather than the Gaussian side matrices of Sections <a href="#sec:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gaussian">4</a> and <a href="#sec:distance-bins" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:distance-bins">5</a>. We express the replica measure by successive simplex heights. The normalization changes at each insertion because the remaining ambient dimension shrinks. A projected-distance distribution-function test, valid even with atoms, recovers a fraction of the height cost. We choose its exponent below one as a function of $d$ and keep the resulting entropy remainder explicit.

Proposition <a href="#haar:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:block">29</a> gives that remainder in terms of the state entropies. In particular, the block cost is $O(d)$ when all state alphabets have logarithmic width $o(d^2)$. We first prove the fixed-prior height identity and cancellation, then average over state values. A Gaussian observation block is converted to its orthonormal row frame only in that final step; the learner’s observations are still the original exact Gaussian pairs.

<a id="haar:dimensions"></a>
<a id="haar:dimension-bounds"></a>
Write $\sigma=\sigma_d$ and $\log^+x=\max\{0,\log x\}$ for $x>0$. A $j$-frame is a $j\times d$ matrix with orthonormal rows; its Haar law is the probability law invariant under orthogonal changes of ambient coordinates. Throughout the section set $$\label{haar:dimensions}
 d\ge64,\qquad k=r=\lfloor d/16\rfloor,\qquad m=r+1,
 \qquad \ell=\lfloor d/4\rfloor.$$ Here $k$ is the actual block row count, $r$ is the number of height insertions, $m=r+1$ is the replica count, and $\ell$ is the side-frame row count. At the $i$th insertion, $1\le i\le r$, the two dimensions that will matter are <a id="haar:dimension-slack"></a>$$\label{haar:dimension-slack}
 n_i=d-i+1,\qquad q_i=\ell-i+1.$$ They satisfy $$\label{haar:dimension-bounds}
 n_i>k+2,\qquad q_i\ge\frac{3d}{16}>k,
 \qquad \frac{q_i}{q_i-k}\le\frac32,
 \qquad \frac{n_i}{q_i}\le\frac{16}{3},\qquad \frac d k\le32.$$ Indeed, $q_i\ge\ell-r+1\ge3d/16$ and $k\le d/16$; also $k\ge d/32$ for $d\ge64$. The other inequalities follow at once. In particular, none of the denominators in <a href="#haar:dimension-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:dimension-bounds">[haar:dimension-bounds]</a> vanishes.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### An exact identity for the replica measure

<a id="haar:sphere-density"></a>
We begin with the normalization of spherical projection. For integers $1\le j<n-2$, put $$\alpha_{n,j}=\frac{\Gamma(n/2)}{\pi^{j/2}\Gamma((n-j)/2)},
 \qquad v_j=\frac{\pi^{j/2}}{\Gamma(j/2+1)}.$$ Here $v_j$ is the volume of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^j$. The first $j$ coordinates of a uniform point of $S^{n-1}$ have density $$\label{haar:sphere-density}
 p_{n,j}(u)=\alpha_{n,j}(1-\lVert u\rVert^2)^{(n-j-2)/2}
            \mathbf 1_{\{\lVert u\rVert<1\}}.$$ Conditional on these coordinates, the remaining direction is uniform, with radius $\sqrt{1-\lVert u\rVert^2}$. These are the density and conditional law in Lemma <a href="#lem:projection-fibers" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:projection-fibers">14</a>, specialized to ambient dimension $n$ and a matrix with orthonormal rows. We keep the ambient dimension in the notation because it changes at each insertion.

<a id="haar:gamma-bounds"></a>
We will also use the elementary estimates $$\label{haar:gamma-bounds}
 \alpha_{n,j}\le n^{j/2},\qquad
 v_j\alpha_{n,j}\le (Cn/j)^{j/2},$$ where $C$ is absolute. For completeness, gamma recursion and $\Gamma(z+1/2)\le\sqrt z\,\Gamma(z)$ imply $\Gamma(n/2)/\Gamma((n-j)/2)\le(n/2)^{j/2}$; the half-step inequality is Cauchy–Schwarz in the gamma integral. Integrating that integral over $[j/2,j/2+1]$ gives $\Gamma(j/2+1)\ge(j/2)^{j/2}e^{-j/2-1}$. These two bounds prove <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a>.

<a id="haar:posterior"></a>
Let $P=g\sigma$ be a probability measure with a bounded Borel density $0\le g\le K<\infty$. Necessarily $K\ge1$. Draw an independent Haar $k$-frame $A$ and $S\sim P$, and write $Y=AS$. Under $\sigma$, the density of $Y$ is $$p^0(y)=\alpha_{d,k}(1-\lVert y\rVert^2)^{(d-k-2)/2}
        \mathbf 1_{\{\lVert y\rVert<1\}}.$$ For $\lVert y\rVert<1$, let $U_{A,y}$ be uniform probability on the sphere $\{s\in S^{d-1}:As=y\}$. On $\lVert y\rVert\ge1$, choose any fixed measurable probability kernel for $U_{A,y}$; it will always be multiplied by $p^0(y)=0$. Fix the following density and posterior versions: $$\label{haar:posterior}
 \begin{split}
 p_A(y)&=p^0(y)\int g\,\mathrm dU_{A,y}
             \quad (\lVert y\rVert<1),\qquad
 p_A(y)=0\quad (\lVert y\rVert\ge1),\\
 P_{A,y}(\mathrm ds)&=
    \frac{g(s)}{\int g\,\mathrm dU_{A,y}}\,U_{A,y}(\mathrm ds)
       \quad\text{when }p_A(y)>0.
 \end{split}$$ The posterior on the unused set $\{p_A=0\}$ may be any fixed measurable probability kernel. These are measurable versions. For example, a fiber direction can be generated by projecting a standard Gaussian onto $\ker A$ and normalizing it. If $u$ is that unit direction, the fiber point is $A^{\mathsf T}y+\sqrt{1-\lVert y\rVert^2}\,u$. A fixed coordinate convention handles the null normalization failures. This construction also makes $p_A(y)$ jointly measurable in $(A,y)$. We have $p_A\le Kp^0$ and, for every $A$, $p_A$ is a probability density.

For a tuple $\boldsymbol s=(s_0,\ldots,s_r)$, define $$D_i=\operatorname{span}\{s_1-s_0,\ldots,s_i-s_0\},\qquad D_0=\{0\},
 \qquad
 \delta_i=\operatorname{dist}(s_i-s_0,D_{i-1}).$$ Thus $\delta_i$ is the height added by the $i$th point. Write $\mathrm dA_D$ for Haar probability on the $k$-frames in $D^\perp$. All subspaces arising here have $\dim D^\perp\ge d-r>k$. A measurable version of this frame kernel is obtained by projecting independent standard Gaussians onto $D^\perp$ and applying a fixed Gram–Schmidt rule. We use $\mathrm dA$ when $D=\{0\}$.

<a id="stmt-haar-coarea"></a>
<div id="haar:coarea" class="lemma">

<a id="haar:coarea-identity"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Haar simplex-height identity). *For the dimensions in <a href="#haar:dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:dimensions">[haar:dimensions]</a> and every probability $P=g\sigma$ with $0\le g\le K<\infty$, the following finite measures on the frame and the whole tuple are equal: $$\label{haar:coarea-identity}
 \mathrm dA\int_{\mathbb R^k}p_A(y)^m\,\mathrm dy
       \prod_{i=0}^r P_{A,y}(\mathrm ds_i)
 =\left(\prod_{i=0}^rP(\mathrm ds_i)\right)
       \left(\prod_{i=1}^r\alpha_{d-i+1,k}\delta_i^{-k}\right)
       \mathrm dA_{D_r}.$$ Equality means equality after integration against every bounded Borel function of $(A,\boldsymbol s)$. Define the displayed product density to be zero on degenerate tuples. The measure on the right is supported on tuples with $\dim D_i=i$ for all $1\le i\le r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-coarea"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-coarea</summary>

<a id="haar:small-ball-insertion"></a>
*Proof.* We first take $P=\sigma$. Fix $s_0,\ldots,s_{i-1}$ with $\dim D_{i-1}=i-1$, and put $H=D_{i-1}^\perp$, so $\dim H=n_i=d-i+1$. For a bounded continuous function $\varphi(A,s)$, consider $$\label{haar:small-ball-insertion}
 I_t=\int\mathrm dA_{D_{i-1}}\int\sigma(\mathrm ds)\,
       \varphi(A,s)
       \frac{\mathbf 1_{\{\lVert A(s-s_0)\rVert\le t\}}}{v_kt^k},
       \qquad t>0.$$ We compute its limit as $t\downarrow0$ in both orders of integration.

<a id="haar:first-order-limit"></a>
Fix $A$ first. Disintegration of $\sigma$ under $s\mapsto As$ rewrites the inner integral as the average over the ball of radius $t$ about $As_0$ of $$y\longmapsto p^0(y)\int\varphi(A,s)\,U_{A,y}(\mathrm ds).$$ This function is continuous in the open unit ball. Indeed, use the fiber representation following <a href="#haar:posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:posterior">[haar:posterior]</a> with a common uniform direction and apply bounded convergence. Because $d>k+2$, the factor $p^0$ tends to zero at the boundary, so the product extends continuously by zero to $\mathbb R^k$. The average therefore tends to $$\label{haar:first-order-limit}
 p^0(As_0)\int\varphi(A,s)\,U_{A,As_0}(\mathrm ds).$$ Its absolute value, and that of every preceding average, is at most $\lVert\varphi\rVert_\infty\alpha_{d,k}$. Dominated convergence thus permits the frame integration as well.

For the other order, fix $s$ and put $\delta=\lVert\operatorname{proj}_H(s-s_0)\rVert$. When $\delta>0$, the vector $A(s-s_0)$ under $\mathrm dA_{D_{i-1}}$ has density <a id="haar:insertion-density"></a>$$\label{haar:insertion-density}
 z\longmapsto\delta^{-k}\alpha_{n_i,k}
       \left(1-\frac{\lVert z\rVert^2}{\delta^2}\right)^{(n_i-k-2)/2}
       \mathbf 1_{\{\lVert z\rVert<\delta\}}.$$ This is <a href="#haar:sphere-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:sphere-density">[haar:sphere-density]</a> in the current ambient space $H$, not in the original $d$-dimensional space. Since $n_i>k+2$, the density is bounded by its value $\alpha_{n_i,k}\delta^{-k}$ at zero.

<a id="haar:second-order-limit"></a>
The conditional frame law also has a concrete limit. Put $e=\operatorname{proj}_H(s-s_0)/\delta$ and $w=z/\delta$. If $C$ is a Haar $k$-frame in $H\cap e^\perp$, then the conditional law of $A$ given $A(s-s_0)=z$, for $\lVert w\rVert<1$, is the law of <a id="haar:conditional-frame"></a>$$\label{haar:conditional-frame}
 A=w e^{\mathsf T}+(I_k-ww^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}C.$$ Its rows are orthonormal and $Ae=w$. Invariance under rotations fixing $e$ identifies the remaining factor as Haar; conversely the displayed formula describes every such frame. The formula is continuous at $w=0$. At zero the frame law is $\mathrm dA_{D_i}$, since $H\cap e^\perp=D_i^\perp$. It follows that the frame integral in <a href="#haar:small-ball-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:small-ball-insertion">[haar:small-ball-insertion]</a> tends, for this $s$, to $$\label{haar:second-order-limit}
 \alpha_{n_i,k}\delta^{-k}
       \int\varphi(A,s)\,\mathrm dA_{D_i}.$$

The bound $\lVert\varphi\rVert_\infty\alpha_{n_i,k}\delta^{-k}$ dominates the frame averages for every $t$. It is integrable in $s$. Choose a $(k+1)$-dimensional subspace $L\subset(D_{i-1}+\operatorname{span}\{s_0\})^\perp$; the available dimension is at least $d-i\ge d-r>k+1$. Then $\delta\ge\lVert\operatorname{proj}_L s\rVert$. The latter vector has a bounded density by <a href="#haar:sphere-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:sphere-density">[haar:sphere-density]</a>, because $d>k+3$. In $k+1$ dimensions its inverse $k$th power is integrable at zero: the radial integral is a constant times $\int_0^1 u^k u^{-k}\,\mathrm du<\infty$. This also shows that $\delta>0$ for $\sigma$-almost every $s$. Dominated convergence proves the second limit after integration in $s$.

Equating the two integrated limits in <a href="#haar:first-order-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:first-order-limit">[haar:first-order-limit]</a> and <a href="#haar:second-order-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:second-order-limit">[haar:second-order-limit]</a> gives the insertion identity. Bounded continuous functions determine finite Borel measures on the compact frame–sphere space, so the identity also holds for bounded Borel tests. The frame and fiber constructions above make the kernels measurable in the preceding tuple, allowing successive integration.

Start with $\sigma(\mathrm ds_0)\mathrm dA$ and insert, for $i=1,\ldots,r$, the kernels $p^0(As_0)U_{A,As_0}(\mathrm ds_i)$. Each insertion changes the frame law from $\mathrm dA_{D_{i-1}}$ to $\mathrm dA_{D_i}$ and contributes $\alpha_{d-i+1,k}\delta_i^{-k}$. On the original side, disintegration of $s_0$ contributes one more factor $p^0(y)$, so its total power is $r+1=m$. These measures are finite at every step because $p^0\le\alpha_{d,k}$; in particular their final mass is $\int p^0(y)^m\,\mathrm dy\le\alpha_{d,k}^{m-1}$. The preceding almost-sure positivity of every inserted height gives the full-rank support. This proves <a href="#haar:coarea-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:coarea-identity">[haar:coarea-identity]</a> for $\sigma$.

Finally multiply this equality of measures by the bounded Borel function $\prod_{i=0}^r g(s_i)$. Formula <a href="#haar:posterior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:posterior">[haar:posterior]</a> changes the left side into the stated measure for $P$, while the right side becomes the product of $P$ measures in <a href="#haar:coarea-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:coarea-identity">[haar:coarea-identity]</a>. The bounded tilt preserves finiteness. No continuity of $g$ or of $p_A$ was used. ◻

</details>

We now pass from the finite measure to the actual posterior experiment. Draw $(A,Y)$ with law $\mathrm dA\,p_A(y)\mathrm dy$, and, conditionally on $(A,Y)$, draw $S_0,\ldots,S_r$ independently from $P_{A,Y}$. Write $Q$ for the law including the frame and tuple, and $Q_S$ for its tuple marginal. Each $(A,S_i)$ has law $\mathrm dA\,P(\mathrm ds)$, and all the replicas satisfy $AS_i=Y$ almost surely.

<a id="haar:replica-density"></a>
Define the probability measure $\Lambda(\mathrm dA,\mathrm d\boldsymbol s)
 =P^{\otimes m}(\mathrm d\boldsymbol s)\mathrm dA_{D_r}$. The degenerate tuples have zero $P^{\otimes m}$-measure, by the positivity proved during insertion; fix any measurable convention there. Multiply <a href="#haar:coarea-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:coarea-identity">[haar:coarea-identity]</a> by $p_A(As_0)^{-r}$ on its positive set, and by zero on its zero set. This operation is justified first for the minimum with a finite constant and then by monotone convergence. On the left it changes $p_A^m$ to $p_A$, since $m=r+1$ and the used posterior is supported on $As_0=y$. Consequently $$\label{haar:replica-density}
 \frac{\mathrm dQ}{\mathrm d\Lambda}(A,\boldsymbol s)
 =\left(\prod_{i=1}^r\alpha_{d-i+1,k}\delta_i^{-k}\right)
       p_A(As_0)^{-r}$$ on the positive-density, full-rank set, with value zero elsewhere.

For a fresh Haar $\ell$-frame $B$, independent of this whole experiment, put $Z_i=BS_i$ and let $P_B$ be the image of $P$ under $B$. Define the two total correlations <a id="haar:correlations"></a>$$\label{haar:correlations}
 J_S=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(Q_S\Vert P^{\otimes m}),\qquad
 J_Z=\mathbb E_B D_{\mathrm{KL}}
       (Q_{Z_0,\ldots,Z_r\mid B}\Vert P_B^{\otimes m}).$$

<a id="stmt-haar-upper-correlation"></a>
<div id="haar:upper-correlation" class="lemma">

<a id="haar:upper-U"></a>
<a id="haar:crude-U"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Finite upper correlation). *The correlations satisfy $0\le J_Z\le J_S\le U<\infty$, where $$\begin{aligned}
 U&=\sum_{i=1}^r\left[
       \log\alpha_{d-i+1,k}+k\mathbb E_Q\log(1/\delta_i)
       -\mathbb E_{A,S\sim P}\log p_A(AS)\right],
       \label{haar:upper-U}\\
 0\le U&\le Crd(\log K+\log d).\label{haar:crude-U}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\mathbb E_{A,S\sim P}$ draws the Haar frame independently of a vector with law $P$. Every logarithm in <a href="#haar:upper-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:upper-U">[haar:upper-U]</a> is integrable.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-upper-correlation"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-upper-correlation</summary>

<a id="haar:density-entropy"></a>
*Proof.* The positive part of $\log p_A$ under $p_A$ is bounded uniformly in $A$ by $\log^+(K\alpha_{d,k})$. Its negative part is also integrable uniformly: the density is supported in a ball of finite volume, and $-x\log x\le1/e$ for $0<x\le1$. Comparison with uniform probability on the unit ball now gives $$\label{haar:density-entropy}
 -\mathbb E_{A,S\sim P}\log p_A(AS)\le\log v_k.$$

To control a height, condition on $(A,Y,S_0,\ldots,S_{i-1})$. Put $h=\sqrt{1-\lVert Y\rVert^2}$ and $n=d-k$. Almost surely $h>0$. The conditional residual direction of $S_i$ in $\ker A$ has density at most $$K'=\frac{Kp^0(Y)}{p_A(Y)}\ge1$$ relative to uniform probability on $S^{n-1}$. Choose a unit vector $e\in\ker A$ perpendicular to the preceding residual directions, including that of $S_0$. There are at most $i\le r<n$ such directions; a fixed Gram–Schmidt rule makes the choice measurable. Then $e\perp D_{i-1}$. If $U_i$ is the new unit residual direction, then $$\delta_i\ge |\langle e,S_i-S_0\rangle|
             =h|\langle e,U_i\rangle|.$$ The one-coordinate case of <a href="#haar:sphere-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:sphere-density">[haar:sphere-density]</a> and <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a> yield $$\mathbb P\{|\langle e,U_i\rangle|\le t
       \mid A,Y,S_0,\ldots,S_{i-1}\}
 \le\min(1,CK'\sqrt d\,t).$$ Integrating this tail with $t=e^{-u}$ shows that the conditional expectation of $\log(1/|\langle e,U_i\rangle|)$ is at most $1+\log(CK'\sqrt d)$.

For fixed $A$, the expected density tilt is $$\mathbb E[\log K'\mid A]
  =\log K-D_{\mathrm{KL}}(p_A\Vert p^0)\le\log K.$$ The divergence is finite: $p_A/p^0\le K$ controls its positive part, and $-x\log x\le1/e$ controls its negative part under $p^0$. Also the individual marginal $(A,S_0)$ consists of an independent Haar frame and a unit vector. Thus $\lVert AS_0\rVert^2$ has beta parameters $k/2,(d-k)/2$ for every fixed target, with both parameters positive. The beta integral gives <a id="haar:radius-moment"></a>$$\label{haar:radius-moment}
 \mathbb E h^{-2}=\frac{d-2}{d-k-2}\le C,
 \qquad \mathbb E\log(1/h)\le\tfrac12\log\mathbb E h^{-2}\le C.$$ Combining these estimates gives $\mathbb E\log(1/\delta_i)\le C+\log K+C\log d$. The negative part is bounded because $\delta_i\le2$. Both signs of every height logarithm are therefore integrable.

We may now take the expected logarithm of <a href="#haar:replica-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:replica-density">[haar:replica-density]</a>. It is exactly the finite relative entropy $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(Q\Vert\Lambda)=U$. Forgetting $A$ by data processing yields $J_S\le U$. Adding the independent $B$ and then projecting coordinatewise yields $J_Z\le J_S$. Finally <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a>, <a href="#haar:density-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:density-entropy">[haar:density-entropy]</a>, and the height bound give <a href="#haar:crude-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:crude-U">[haar:crude-U]</a>. ◻

</details>

The inverse-height terms in the upper bound can grow with the density bound $K$. We will keep those terms until the projected correlation has supplied matching lower bounds. This order is what permits averaging over rare states at the end.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The correlation retained by the auxiliary projection

We will bound the probability that an independent projected signal lies close to the affine span of preceding projected replicas. Averaging over the side frame turns this tube probability into ball averages of $p_A$ around the actual label $AS_0$. Their radii vary with the simplex height; the next lemma controls the logarithmic cost of taking a supremum over those radii.

For a probability density $f$ on $\mathbb R^k$, define its centered maximal average by $$\mathcal M f(u)=\sup_{t>0}\frac{1}{v_kt^k}
        \int_{\lVert u'-u\rVert\le t} f(u')\,\mathrm du'.$$ For fixed $f\in L^1$, the ball integral is continuous in its center and positive radius; the supremum may therefore be restricted to positive rational radii. In particular $\mathcal M f$ is measurable. For the jointly measurable family $p_A$, the same rational supremum and Tonelli’s theorem give joint measurability in $(A,u)$.

For historical background on one-dimensional maximal averages, see Hardy and Littlewood (Hardy and Littlewood 1930, sec. III) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[HardyLittlewood1930](#ref-HardyLittlewood1930)]. Stein and Strömberg (Stein and Strömberg 1983, 259) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[SteinStromberg1983](#ref-SteinStromberg1983)] discuss the exponential dimension cost of customary covering proofs. We prove the centered weak estimate needed here, including its constant.

<a id="stmt-haar-maximal"></a>
<div id="haar:maximal" class="lemma">

<a id="haar:maximal-log"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Logarithmic maximal estimate). *Suppose $f$ is a probability density supported in the unit ball of $\mathbb R^k$ and $f\le K\alpha_{d,k}$. Then $$\label{haar:maximal-log}
 \int f(u)\log\frac{\mathcal M f(u)}{f(u)}\,\mathrm du
 \le Cd+\log(1+Cd+\log K).$$ Both logarithms are integrable under $f(u)\mathrm du$. The integrand is taken as zero on $\{f=0\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-maximal</summary>

<a id="haar:weak-maximal"></a>
*Proof.* For every $\lambda>0$, $$\label{haar:weak-maximal}
 \bigl|\{u:\mathcal M f(u)>\lambda\}\bigr|
       \le\frac{3^k}{\lambda}.$$ To prove this, take a compact subset of the displayed superlevel set. Open and closed balls have the same $f$-mass, since their boundaries have Lebesgue measure zero. At each point choose an open ball centered there whose $f$-mass exceeds $\lambda$ times its volume. The strict inequality persists under a sufficiently small enlargement, so these balls give an open cover. Take a finite subcover and greedily keep disjoint balls in decreasing order of radius. Every discarded ball meets a retained ball of at least its radius, and is contained in that ball’s threefold dilation. Hence the compact set has volume at most $3^k$ times the sum of the retained volumes. The retained balls are disjoint and have total $f$-mass at most one, so their volumes sum to less than $1/\lambda$. Inner regularity proves <a href="#haar:weak-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:weak-maximal">[haar:weak-maximal]</a>.

Set $b=3^k$ and $L_f=K\alpha_{d,k}$. Since $\mathcal M f\le L_f$, layer integration over the unit ball gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{\lVert u\rVert\le1}\mathcal M f(u)\,\mathrm du
 &\le\int_0^{L_f}\min(v_k,b/\lambda)\,\mathrm d\lambda\\
 &\le b\bigl(1+\log^+(L_fv_k/b)\bigr).
\end{aligned}$$ On the support of $f$, a radius-two ball contains the whole unit ball, so $\mathcal M f\ge(v_k2^k)^{-1}$ there. Its logarithm is bounded on that support, while $f\log f$ is integrable by the bounded-density argument in Lemma <a href="#haar:upper-correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:upper-correlation">26</a>. Jensen’s inequality under the probability density $f$ now yields $$\int f\log(\mathcal M f/f)
 \le\log\int_{\{f>0\}}\mathcal M f
 \le k\log3+\log\bigl(1+\log^+(K\alpha_{d,k}v_k/3^k)\bigr).$$ Use <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a> and $d/k\le32$ to obtain <a href="#haar:maximal-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:maximal-log">[haar:maximal-log]</a>. ◻

</details>

The next test uses only the projected points, including when their conditional distance distribution has atoms.

<a id="stmt-haar-lower-correlation"></a>
<div id="haar:lower-correlation" class="lemma">

<a id="haar:lower-Z"></a>
**Lemma 28** (Lower projected correlation). *For the replica law above and every $0<a<1$, $$\label{haar:lower-Z}
 J_Z\ge a\sum_{i=1}^r\left[
       k\mathbb E_Q\log(1/\delta_i)-\log(C_1^kv_k)
       -\mathbb E_{A,S\sim P}\log\mathcal M p_A(AS)\right]
       +r\log(1-a),$$ where $C_1$ is an absolute constant, independent of $P,K,d,a$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-lower-correlation"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-lower-correlation</summary>

*Proof.* Given $(B,Z_0,\ldots,Z_{i-1})$, define for $z\in\mathbb R^\ell$ $$R_i(z)=\operatorname{dist}\bigl(z-Z_0,
             \operatorname{span}\{Z_j-Z_0:1\le j<i\}\bigr).$$ Let $F_i(t)=P\{s:R_i(Bs)\le t\}$ be its distribution function under an independent $s\sim P$. The distance, and hence this conditional distribution function, is measurable in the projected history: the distance is the infimum of the corresponding norms over rational coefficient vectors. Let $\rho_i=R_i(Z_i)$ be the actual replica distance.

<a id="haar:superuniform"></a>
<a id="haar:cdf-moment"></a>
If a random variable $R$ has distribution function $F$, then $$\label{haar:superuniform}
 \mathbb P\{F(R)\le u\}\le u\qquad(0\le u\le1).$$ This includes distributions with atoms. In fact, the set of real $x$ with $F(x)\le u$ is an initial interval, whose probability is the supremum of $F$ on that interval and is at most $u$. Tail integration of <a href="#haar:superuniform" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:superuniform">[haar:superuniform]</a> gives, for $0<a<1$, $$\label{haar:cdf-moment}
 \mathbb E F(R)^{-a}
 \le1+\int_1^\infty t^{-1/a}\,\mathrm dt=\frac1{1-a}.$$ In particular $F(R)>0$ almost surely under its own law.

<a id="haar:cdf-test"></a>
Apply the relative-entropy variational inequality to the test $z\mapsto F_i(R_i(z))^{-a}$ under the conditional one-vector reference law $P_B$. For an unbounded test, use its minimum with a finite constant and pass monotonically to the limit; <a href="#haar:cdf-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:cdf-moment">[haar:cdf-moment]</a> bounds its reference expectation. The chain rule for $J_Z$ has one term for each conditional law of $Z_i$ given $(B,Z_0,\ldots,Z_{i-1})$, and its $i=0$ term is zero. It follows that $$\label{haar:cdf-test}
 J_Z\ge a\sum_{i=1}^r\mathbb E_{Q,B}\log\frac1{F_i(\rho_i)}
             +r\log(1-a).$$ The logarithmic expectations are finite: the variational bound after averaging controls their nonnegative parts by the finite correlation $J_Z\le J_S$ from Lemma <a href="#haar:upper-correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:upper-correlation">26</a>.

We next estimate $F_i(\delta_i)$ while holding the entire replica outcome $(A,S_0,\ldots,S_r)$ fixed. Put $D=D_{i-1}$ and $E=\operatorname{row}(B)$. The isometry $B^{\mathsf T}:\mathbb R^\ell
\to E$ gives the exact identity <a id="haar:intersection"></a>$$\label{haar:intersection}
 \operatorname{dist}(Bu,BD)
       =\lVert\operatorname{proj}_{E\cap D^\perp}u\rVert.$$ Indeed, the orthogonal complement within $E$ of $\operatorname{proj}_E D$ is precisely $E\cap D^\perp$. The projection of the fixed $(i-1)$-space $D$ into a Haar $\ell$-space $E$ has full rank almost surely: generate $E$ with independent standard Gaussians and restrict them to an orthonormal basis of $D$, obtaining an $\ell\times(i-1)$ Gaussian matrix of full column rank. Thus $\dim(E\cap D^\perp)=q_i=\ell-i+1$. Rotations preserving $D$ show that this intersection is a uniform $q_i$-space in the $n_i$-space $D^\perp$.

<a id="haar:projected-small-ball"></a>
For fixed $u$, let $v=\operatorname{proj}_{D^\perp}u$. Spherical projection in that $n_i$-space, followed by <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a>, gives $$\label{haar:projected-small-ball}
 \mathbb P_B\{\operatorname{dist}(Bu,BD)\le t\}
 \le\min\left(1,\left(\frac{C_0t}{\lVert v\rVert}\right)^{q_i}\right),$$ with value one if $v=0$. The constant $C_0$ is absolute because $n_i/q_i\le16/3$; the density formula is applicable because $n_i-q_i=d-\ell>2$. In the replica experiment $A$ annihilates $D$. Therefore for $u=s-S_0$, $\lVert v\rVert\ge\lVert A(s-S_0)\rVert$. Also $\rho_i\le\delta_i$, since $B$ is a contraction and the distance minimization permits all vectors in $D$.

<a id="haar:projected-maximal"></a>
Set $u_0=AS_0$ and $q=q_i$. The identity $$\min(1,(b/x)^q)=\int_1^\infty qt^{-q-1}
                 \mathbf 1_{\{x\le bt\}}\,\mathrm dt
       \qquad(b>0,\ x\ge0)$$ also holds at $x=0$. Integrating <a href="#haar:projected-small-ball" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:projected-small-ball">[haar:projected-small-ball]</a> against $s\sim P$ and using Tonelli’s theorem yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_B F_i(\delta_i)
 &\le\int_1^\infty qt^{-q-1}
       P\{\lVert As-u_0\rVert\le C_0\delta_i t\}\,\mathrm dt\notag\\
 &\le v_k(C_0\delta_i)^k\mathcal M p_A(u_0)
             \int_1^\infty qt^{k-q-1}\,\mathrm dt\notag\\
 &=\frac{q_i}{q_i-k}v_k(C_0\delta_i)^k\mathcal M p_A(u_0)
 \le C_1^kv_k\delta_i^k\mathcal M p_A(u_0).
 \label{haar:projected-maximal}
\end{aligned}$$ The integral converges because $q_i>k$, and the last step uses the exact bound $q_i/(q_i-k)\le3/2$. One absolute choice of $C_1$ works for every $k\ge1$.

Now $F_i(\rho_i)\le F_i(\delta_i)$. Jensen’s inequality for $-\log$ and <a href="#haar:projected-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:projected-maximal">[haar:projected-maximal]</a>, first for each fixed replica outcome, give $$\mathbb E_{Q,B}\log\frac1{F_i(\rho_i)}
 \ge k\mathbb E_Q\log(1/\delta_i)-\log(C_1^kv_k)
       -\mathbb E_{A,S\sim P}\log\mathcal M p_A(AS).$$ Here the last expectation uses the individual marginal $(A,S_0)$. The right side is finite by Lemmas <a href="#haar:upper-correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:upper-correlation">26</a> and <a href="#haar:maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:maximal">27</a>; Jensen may also be read in the extended sense before averaging. Substitution into <a href="#haar:cdf-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:cdf-test">[haar:cdf-test]</a> proves <a href="#haar:lower-Z" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:lower-Z">[haar:lower-Z]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Cancellation and the block information bound

<a id="haar:cancellation"></a>
<a id="haar:fixed-prior-budget"></a>
The preceding estimates can now be subtracted. Retaining every term of $U$ gives, for every $0<a<1$, $$\begin{aligned}
 J_S-J_Z\le{}&(1-a)U-r\log(1-a)\notag\\
 &+a\sum_{i=1}^r\left[
       \log(\alpha_{d-i+1,k}C_1^kv_k)
       +\mathbb E_{A,S\sim P}
          \log\frac{\mathcal M p_A(AS)}{p_A(AS)}\right].
 \label{haar:cancellation}
\end{aligned}$$ Thus only the fraction $a$ of the height contribution has been canceled; the residual $(1-a)U$ remains explicit. Take <a id="haar:a-choice"></a>$$\label{haar:a-choice}
 a=1-d^{-3}.$$ Using <a href="#haar:crude-U" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:crude-U">[haar:crude-U]</a>, <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a>, and Lemma <a href="#haar:maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:maximal">27</a> in <a href="#haar:cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:cancellation">[haar:cancellation]</a> yields $$\label{haar:fixed-prior-budget}
 \frac{J_S-J_Z}{m}
 \le Cd+\log(1+Cd+\log K)
       +C\frac{\log K+\log d}{d^2}.$$ In detail, $(1-a)U/m\le C(\log K+\log d)/d^2$ because $r/m\le1$, and $-r\log(1-a)/m\le3\log d$. Also $\log(\alpha_{n_i,k}C_1^kv_k)\le Cd$ by <a href="#haar:gamma-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:gamma-bounds">[haar:gamma-bounds]</a> and $n_i/k\le32$. These are all the terms in <a href="#haar:cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:cancellation">[haar:cancellation]</a>; the $3\log d$ term is absorbed into $Cd$.

<a id="stmt-haar-block"></a>
<div id="haar:block" class="proposition">

<a id="haar:state-budget"></a>
**Proposition 29** (Haar block information bound). *Let $S\sim\sigma$, let $V$ be a finite-valued state, and let a block consist of $k=\lfloor d/16\rfloor$ fresh independent standard Gaussian designs, independent of $(S,V)$ and the past, with their exact labels.
