Suppose a finite-valued state $W$ is generated by a measurable randomized kernel from $V$ and the full block, with no other retained information from the past. Let $B$ be a Haar $\ell=\lfloor d/4\rfloor$ frame drawn independently of the entire joint experiment producing $S,V$, the block, and $W$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 I(S;W\mid V,B,BS)\le{}&\frac{H(W\mid V)}{m}+Cd
       +\log(1+Cd+H(V))\notag\\
 &+C\frac{H(V)+\log d}{d^2},
 \qquad m=\lfloor d/16\rfloor+1.
 \label{haar:state-budget}
\end{aligned}$$ The constant $C$ is absolute. If every state has logarithmic width at most $H_*=o(d^2)$, the left side is at most $Cd$ for all sufficiently large $d$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-block"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-block</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a state value $v$ with probability $p_v>0$. Its conditional signal law has the bounded density $$g_v(s)=\frac{\mathbb P\{V=v\mid S=s\}}{p_v}\le K_v:=\frac1{p_v}$$ relative to $\sigma$. The new Gaussian design matrix is independent of $(S,V)$. Its row Gram–Schmidt factorization is $RA$ with an invertible triangular $R$ and a Haar $k$-frame $A$, almost surely. The exact labels are $R(AS)$, so, conditional on the design, observing them is equivalent to observing $Y=AS$. Because the whole design is independent of $S$ conditional on $V=v$, its coefficients $R$ add no information about $S$ once $(A,Y)$ is specified.

Generate the replicas conditionally independently from this posterior, and independently of $W$ given $v$ and the complete block data. A single replica then has the same joint law with the block data and $W$ as the original signal: both use the posterior given the block, and $W$ has no further dependence on the signal once those data and $v$ are fixed. In particular each pair $(S_i,W)$ has the same conditional law as $(S,W)$, the auxiliary $B$ is independent of the entire tuple and $W$, and the original pair used on the left is also coupled independently with $B$. The common map $(B,s)\mapsto Bs$ is continuous, and the unprojected total correlation is finite by Lemma <a href="#haar:upper-correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:upper-correlation">26</a>. These are precisely the application hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-information">13</a>. Applied in the conditional experiment $V=v$, it gives $$mI(S;W\mid V=v,B,BS)
 \le H(W\mid V=v)+J_S(v)-J_Z(v).$$ No conditional independence of the replicas given $W$ is asserted or needed.

Insert <a href="#haar:fixed-prior-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:fixed-prior-budget">[haar:fixed-prior-budget]</a> with $K=K_v$ and average over $v$. The identity $$\sum_v p_v\log K_v=\sum_vp_v\log(1/p_v)=H(V)$$ accounts exactly for rare state values. Jensen’s inequality bounds the average of $\log(1+Cd+\log K_v)$ by $\log(1+Cd+H(V))$. This proves <a href="#haar:state-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:state-budget">[haar:state-budget]</a>. Under the width hypothesis, $H(W\mid V)/m=o(d)$, the last term is $o(1)$, and the logarithmic term is $O(\log d)$ for all sufficiently large $d$. The stated $Cd$ bound follows. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### The terminal fiber and the streaming conclusion

The block comparison is complete. We apply the residual-sphere endpoint of Section <a href="#sec:model-endpoint" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:model-endpoint">2</a> with the present Haar side frame, then compare the resulting information with the number of blocks.

<a id="stmt-haar-terminal"></a>
<div id="haar:terminal" class="lemma">

<a id="haar:terminal-information"></a>
**Lemma 30** (The terminal Haar fiber). *Let $S\sim\sigma$, let $V_f$ be finite-valued, and let the unit-valued output $\widehat S$ be generated from $V_f$ by a measurable kernel using fresh randomness, conditionally independently of $S$. Let $B$ be a Haar $\ell=\lfloor d/4\rfloor$ frame independent of $(S,V_f,\widehat S)$. If $\mathbb P\{\arccos\langle\widehat S,S\rangle\le\epsilon\}\ge2/3$ for $0<\epsilon\le1/10$, then $$\label{haar:terminal-information}
 I(S;V_f\mid B,BS)
 \ge\frac{d-\ell-1}{3}\log\frac1{4\epsilon}-\log2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-terminal"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-terminal</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:residual-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residual-information">6</a> with $G=B$, $V=V_f$, $b=\ell$, and $r_0=1/2$. Its hypotheses hold because $B$ is independent of $(S,V_f,\widehat S)$. Explicitly, the Markov bound for the residual radius $h$ is $\mathbb P\{h<1/2\}\le4\ell/(3d)\le1/3$. Success together with $h\ge1/2$ therefore has probability at least $1/3$, while the conditional prior cap probability is at most $(4\epsilon)^{d-\ell-1}$. These are exactly the constants in <a href="#haar:terminal-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:terminal-information">[haar:terminal-information]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-haar-streaming"></a>
<div id="haar:streaming" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31** (Streaming consequence). *For each dimension $d$, let $S\sim\sigma$ and consider a learner in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a>, with deterministic horizon $T=T(d)$ and nonnegative integer memory $M=M(d)$. If $M(d)=o(d^2)$, $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$, and its angular success probability averaged over the signal, samples, and learner randomness is at least $2/3$, then $$T(d)=\Omega\bigl(d\log(1/\epsilon(d))\bigr).$$ The implicit constant is absolute; the dimension threshold may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-haar-streaming"><summary>Proof of stmt-haar-streaming</summary>

*Proof.* Apply the fixed-shared-seed part of Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> to the learner in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a>. The resulting Borel experiment has data-independent initialization and uniform-prior success at least $2/3$, with fresh transition and output randomness retained. This is the success threshold in Lemma <a href="#haar:terminal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:terminal">30</a>.

First, success forces $T=\Omega(d)$ without any memory restriction. Suppose $T\le d/8$, and grant an estimator all $T$ raw pairs, filling in ignored samples after any early stopping. Lemma <a href="#lem:residual-sphere" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residual-sphere">5</a> with $b=T$ gives a uniform residual sphere in kernel dimension $d-T$; <a href="#eq:residual-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-markov">[eq:residual-markov]</a> bounds its probability of radius below $1/2$ by $4T/(3d)$. At $T=0$ the radius is one deterministically. On radius at least $1/2$, the cap argument of Lemma <a href="#lem:raw-sample-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:raw-sample-bound">12</a> bounds conditional success by $(4\epsilon)^{d-T-1}$. Thus <a id="haar:raw-sample-bound"></a>$$\label{haar:raw-sample-bound}
 \mathbb P\{\text{angular error}\le\epsilon\}
 \le\frac{4T}{3d}+(4\epsilon)^{d-T-1}
       \qquad(T\le d/8).$$ The output has no further dependence on $S$ conditional on all the raw data, so the bound also covers randomized estimators. Its right side is at most $1/6+(0.4)^{7d/8-1}<2/3$ for all sufficiently large $d$. Hence $T>d/8$ on those dimensions.

<a id="haar:output-count"></a>
Put $L=\log(1/\epsilon)\ge\log10$. If $T\ge dL$, the conclusion already holds. On the remaining branch $T<dL$, apply the output-capacity bound of Lemma <a href="#lem:early-clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:early-clock">11</a> to this uniform-prior experiment with $p_0=2/3$. It gives $$\label{haar:output-count}
 (d-1)L\le M\log2+\log(T+1)+\log(3/2).$$ For large $d$, $\log(T+1)\le\log(dL+1)\le dL/2$: here $dL\ge d\log10$ and $\log(x+1)\le x/2$ for $x\ge3$. Thus <a href="#haar:output-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:output-count">[haar:output-count]</a> implies <a id="haar:short-clock"></a>$$\label{haar:short-clock}
 dL\le C(M+1),\qquad L=o(d),\qquad T=o(d^2).$$ For example, after subtracting $dL/2$, the left side is $(d/2-1)L\ge dL/4$ for $d\ge4$. The second and third conclusions follow from $M=o(d^2)$ and $T<dL$.

<a id="haar:padded-width"></a>
Use Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> to retain the stopping index, postpone the fresh randomized output, and append ignored rows through the last block. Its state count gives every padded state entropy at most $$\label{haar:padded-width}
 H_*=M\log2+\log(T+2)
 \le M\log2+\log(C(M+1)+2)=o(d^2).$$ This count restricts the branch where the block argument is needed; it does not replace that argument in the representable-precision regime.

<a id="haar:information-clock"></a>
Use a single independent Haar $\ell$-frame $B$ for the entire analysis, put $Z=BS$, and divide the padded stream into $N=\lceil T/k\rceil$ blocks. For consecutive states $V,W$, the chain rule and data processing give $$I(S;W\mid B,Z)
 \le I(S;V,W\mid B,Z)
 =I(S;V\mid B,Z)+I(S;W\mid V,B,Z).$$ The initial state is independent of $(S,B,Z)$. Proposition <a href="#haar:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:block">29</a> and <a href="#haar:padded-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:padded-width">[haar:padded-width]</a> therefore imply $$\label{haar:information-clock}
 I(S;V_f\mid B,Z)\le Cd\lceil T/k\rceil.$$ The terminal lower bound <a href="#haar:terminal-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:terminal-information">[haar:terminal-information]</a> is at least $c dL$ for all sufficiently large $d$: indeed $d-\ell-1\ge d/2$ and $$\log\frac1{4\epsilon}
 \ge\left(1-\frac{\log4}{\log10}\right)L,$$ while the subtractive $\log2$ is absorbed because $dL\to\infty$. Finally $k\ge d/32$ and the already proved $T>d/8$ give $$d\lceil T/k\rceil\le32T+d\le40T.$$ Comparison with <a href="#haar:information-clock" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="haar:information-clock">[haar:information-clock]</a> proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

A learner that succeeds with probability at least $2/3$ for every fixed signal also has uniform-prior success at least $2/3$ by integration. Thus Proposition <a href="#haar:streaming" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="haar:streaming">31</a> separately gives the corresponding worst-signal lower bound, with the same exact observations and finite-state model.

<a id="sec:synthetic"></a>
### Synthetic Gaussian projections

The Gaussian test in Section <a href="#sec:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gaussian">4</a> uses an orthonormal selector of the projected difference space’s complement. Here we instead draw additional Gaussian rows in that complement. Their composition with the side matrix has a random covariance, which must be averaged in the likelihood comparison. To normalize that comparison, we apply the Gaussian-base equal-label identity to the projected prior, rather than applying only its spherical instance to the original prior.

The inverse-determinant scale of this random covariance cancels the scale of the projected simplex volume. A critical inverse-distance kernel then gives a double-logarithmic density cost. Although the cost has the same order as in Section <a href="#sec:gaussian" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:gaussian">4</a>, the comparison law and cancellation are different. We use this bound while conditioning on an entire state history.

<a id="syn:parameters"></a>
<a id="syn:dimension-margins"></a>
We use the spherical and Gaussian notation of Section <a href="#sec:replicas" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:replicas">3</a>. Fix $d\ge16$ and put $$\label{syn:parameters}
 k=\ell=\left\lfloor\frac d8\right\rfloor,\qquad
 u=\ell-1,\qquad m=\left\lfloor\frac d2\right\rfloor,\qquad q=m-u.$$ In particular, $$\label{syn:dimension-margins}
 k+u\le m-2,\qquad q-k-1=m-2k>0,\qquad
 d-1-u-k=d-2k\ge\frac{3d}{4},\qquad
 \frac{m}{q-k-1}\le2.$$ The letter $m$ in this section is the dimension of the side projection. The difference count in Theorem <a href="#thm:equal-label-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:equal-label-measure">15</a> will be $u$.

<a id="stmt-syn-block"></a>
<div id="syn:block" class="proposition">

<a id="syn:block-bound"></a>
**Proposition 32** (Information beyond an independent Gaussian projection). *Let $P=f\sigma_d$ be a probability, where $f$ is a nonnegative Borel function satisfying $f\le\Lambda<\infty$, with $\Lambda\ge1$. Draw $X\sim P$ and $A\sim\gamma_{k,d}$ independently. Let $z=z(A,AX)$ be a measurable function taking at most $K\ge1$ values. Independently of this entire experiment draw $B\sim\gamma_{m,d}$, and put $Y=BX$. With the parameters in <a href="#syn:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:parameters">[syn:parameters]</a>, $$\label{syn:block-bound}
 I(X;z\mid B,Y)
 \le \frac{\log K}{\ell}
       +C\bigl[d+\log(1+\log\Lambda)\bigr],$$ where $C$ is absolute and independent of $P,\Lambda,K$, and the message rule.*

</div>

The proof couples $\ell$ signals by giving them the same actual measurements. Corollary <a href="#cor:replica-likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:replica-likelihood">16</a> describes their joint law. We compare its total correlation before and after applying $B$. For the comparison after $B$, a Gaussian matrix is constrained to annihilate the projected differences. We first construct the resulting probability law, then estimate its density while keeping its dependence on $B$ explicit.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### The actual and projected replica laws

For a tuple $\mathbf x=(x_1,\ldots,x_\ell)$ in $\mathbb R^h$, where $h$ will be $d$ or $m$, write $$D_{\mathbf x}=[x_2-x_1,\ldots,x_\ell-x_1],\qquad
 L_{\mathbf x}=\mathop{\mathrm{col}}(D_{\mathbf x}),\qquad
 \Delta(\mathbf x)=\det(D_{\mathbf x}^{\mathsf T}D_{\mathbf x})^{1/2}.$$ Thus $L_{\mathbf x}$ and $\Delta(\mathbf x)$ are the objects $F_{\mathbf x}$ and $J(\mathbf x)$ of <a href="#eq:equal-label-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:equal-label-geometry">[eq:equal-label-geometry]</a>, with difference count $u$. At full affine rank define <a id="syn:tuple-weight"></a>$$\label{syn:tuple-weight}
 w(\mathbf x)=(2\pi)^{-ku/2}\Delta(\mathbf x)^{-k}.$$

We need the same version of a projection density before and after a composition of matrices. If $\nu$ is a finite measure and $C$ has $r$ rows, write <a id="syn:density-version"></a>$$\label{syn:density-version}
 g_C^\nu(v)=\liminf_{j\to\infty}
       \int\varphi_{1/j,r}(Cs-v)\,\nu(ds),\qquad
 \varphi_{\eta,r}(v)=(2\pi\eta^2)^{-r/2}
               \exp\!\left(-\frac{\lVert v\rVert^2}{2\eta^2}\right).$$ This formula defines a jointly measurable function even for matrices of deficient rank. In every use below the matrix has full row rank almost surely, and Theorem <a href="#thm:equal-label-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:equal-label-measure">15</a> identifies this version with the projection density and transfers its finite positive values to the constrained evaluations. We abbreviate $g_C^P$ to $g_C$.

<a id="syn:correlation-upper"></a>
Fix the data of Proposition <a href="#syn:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:block">32</a>. In Corollary <a href="#cor:replica-likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:replica-likelihood">16</a>, take ambient dimension $d$, $k$ rows, difference count $u$, and prior $P$. The condition $k+u\le d-2$ follows from <a href="#syn:dimension-margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:dimension-margins">[syn:dimension-margins]</a>. Draw $A\sim\gamma_{k,d}$, then a common label $b$ with density $g_A$, and finally draw $X_1,\ldots,X_\ell$ independently from $P$ conditional on $AX=b$, and set $z=z(A,b)$. Denote this law by $Q$. Its tuple–matrix likelihood is <a id="syn:actual-replica"></a>$$\label{syn:actual-replica}
 Q(d\mathbf x,dA)
 =P^{\otimes\ell}(d\mathbf x)\gamma_{k,d}^{L_{\mathbf x}}(dA)
       w(\mathbf x)g_A(Ax_1)^{-u}.$$ Every pair $(X_i,z)$ has the original signal–message law. Independently draw $B$ and put $Y_i=BX_i$. For fixed $B$, let $P^B=B_\#P$, and define $$\mathcal C_X=D_{\mathrm{KL}}(Q_{\mathbf X}\Vert P^{\otimes\ell}),\qquad
 \mathcal C_Y=\mathbb E_BD_{\mathrm{KL}}(Q_{\mathbf Y\mid B}\Vert(P^B)^{\otimes\ell}).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a>, with the same substitution and density bound $L=\Lambda$, proves absolute integrability of $\log g_A(b)$ and $\log w(\mathbf X)$. It gives $$\label{syn:correlation-upper}
 \mathcal C_X
 \le \mathbb E\log w(\mathbf X)-u\,\mathbb E\log g_A(b)<\infty.$$ Data processing under the coordinatewise map $x_i\mapsto Bx_i$ gives $0\le\mathcal C_Y\le\mathcal C_X$.

We next construct a probability density against which to test $\mathcal C_Y$. Almost every $B$ has full row rank. For such a fixed $B$, the spherical projection formula <a href="#eq:spherical-projection-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spherical-projection-density">[eq:spherical-projection-density]</a>, with $m$ rows, and $P\le\Lambda\sigma_d$ show that $P^B$ has bounded Lebesgue density on a compact ellipsoid. Here $d-m-2\ge0$. The standard Gaussian density has a positive minimum on that ellipsoid, so $P^B$ has bounded density relative to standard Gaussian probability on $\mathbb R^m$. This bound is finite for each such $B$ and is allowed to depend on $B$.

<a id="syn:comparison-density"></a>
Apply Corollary <a href="#cor:replica-likelihood" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:replica-likelihood">16</a> in ambient dimension $m$, again with $k$ rows and difference count $u$. Its dimension condition is $k+u\le m-2$, as in <a href="#syn:dimension-margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:dimension-margins">[syn:dimension-margins]</a>. The resulting same-label tuple law has density $$\label{syn:comparison-density}
 R_B(\mathbf y)
 =w(\mathbf y)\,
   \mathbb E_{F\sim\gamma_{k,m}^{L_{\mathbf y}}}g_{FB}(Fy_1)^{-u}$$ relative to $(P^B)^{\otimes\ell}$. Indeed, the density version agrees pointwise under composition: <a id="syn:composition-version"></a>$$\label{syn:composition-version}
 g_F^{P^B}(v)
 =\liminf_j\int\varphi_{1/j,k}(Fy-v)\,P^B(dy)
 =g_{FB}^{P}(v).$$ The finite-measure theorem transfers version, zero, and infinity exceptions before the negative power is taken. Thus $R_B$ is finite and positive almost everywhere for the product law and integrates to one. It can be chosen jointly measurable in $B,\mathbf y$: on the full-affine-rank set, realize the projected Gaussian rows with the Borel orthogonal projection onto $L_{\mathbf y}^{\perp}$, and assign fixed values on the null complement.

The actual law $Q_{\mathbf Y\mid B}$ is absolutely continuous relative to $(P^B)^{\otimes\ell}$, because $Q_{\mathbf X}\ll P^{\otimes\ell}$ and $B$ is independent of the tuple. At the actual $(B,\mathbf Y)$, draw $F$ with the conditional law in <a href="#syn:comparison-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:comparison-density">[syn:comparison-density]</a> and put $$\Xi=g_{FB}(FY_1).$$ The same null-set transfer makes $\Xi$ finite and positive almost surely in this enlarged experiment.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### The synthetic density and its critical kernel

The comparison density $R_B$ contains the inverse $u$th moment of $\Xi$. To compare its logarithm with the actual likelihood in <a href="#syn:correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:correlation-upper">[syn:correlation-upper]</a>, we will control $\Xi$ using the actual matrix $A$ and label $b$. The next lemma first averages the synthetic Gaussian covariance at a fixed tuple. It then bounds the resulting inverse-distance integral by a function $U_A(b)$ whose total mass can be estimated independently of that tuple.

<a id="stmt-syn-synthetic"></a>
<div id="syn:synthetic" class="lemma">

<a id="syn:synthetic-bound"></a>
**Lemma 33** (Synthetic likelihood and critical kernel). *Let $P=f\sigma_d$ be as in Proposition <a href="#syn:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:block">32</a>. Fix a tuple $\mathbf x\in(S^{d-1})^\ell$ of affine rank $u$, and put $$x=x_1,\qquad L=L_{\mathbf x},\qquad
 \delta(s)=\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(s-x,L).$$ Draw $B\sim\gamma_{m,d}$. Given $B$, draw $F\sim\gamma_{k,m}^{BL}$, so each row of $F$ is a standard Gaussian in $(BL)^\perp$. Then $$\label{syn:synthetic-bound}
 \mathbb E_{B,F}g_{FB}(FBx)
 \le (2\pi)^{-k/2}a_q\int\delta(s)^{-k}\,P(ds),\qquad
 a_q=\mathbb E\det(HH^{\mathsf T})^{-1/2}\le(q-k-1)^{-k/2},$$ where $H$ is a standard Gaussian $k\times q$ matrix.*

<a id="syn:cutoff"></a>
<a id="syn:majorant"></a>
<a id="syn:kernel-bound"></a>
<a id="syn:majorant-mass"></a>
<a id="syn:majorant-log"></a>
*There is an absolute $C_0>0$ such that the following holds with $$\label{syn:cutoff}
 r_*=\exp\!\left[-\frac{C_0(d+\log\Lambda)}{d-1-u-k}\right]\in(0,1).$$ For every full-row-rank $k\times d$ matrix $A$, put $a=\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ and define $$\begin{aligned}
 U_A(b)={}&(2\pi)^{-k/2}\mathbf 1_{\{\lVert b\rVert\le a\}}
 \left[1+a^k\int g_A(v)\mathbf 1_{\{\lVert v-b\rVert\le2a\}}\right. \notag\\[-2mm]
 &\hspace{42mm}\left.
       {}\cdot\min\{(ar_*)^{-k},\lVert v-b\rVert^{-k}\}\,dv\right].
 \label{syn:majorant}
\end{aligned}$$ At $v=b$, the second entry in the minimum is interpreted as $+\infty$. If $Ax=b$ and $L\subset\ker A$, then $$\label{syn:kernel-bound}
 (2\pi)^{-k/2}\int\delta(s)^{-k}\,P(ds)\le U_A(b).$$ Writing $V_k$ for the volume of the Euclidean unit ball in $\mathbb R^k$, the same function satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \int U_A(b)\,db
 &\le (2\pi)^{-k/2}V_k a^k[2+k\log(2/r_*)],
 \label{syn:majorant-mass}\\
 \mathbb E_{A\sim\gamma_{k,d}}\log\int U_A(b)\,db
 &\le C\bigl[d+\log(1+\log\Lambda)\bigr].
 \label{syn:majorant-log}
\end{aligned}$$ The logarithm in the last display is absolutely integrable, and $\mathbb E_A\log(1+\int U_A)<\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-syn-synthetic"><summary>Proof of stmt-syn-synthetic</summary>

<a id="syn:covariance"></a>
*Proof.* At the fixed tuple, the restriction of $B$ to $L$ has rank $u$ almost surely. Choose an orthonormal $m\times q$ matrix $N$ whose columns span $(BL)^\perp$, using only $BD_{\mathbf x}$. Gram–Schmidt with a fixed ordering gives a measurable choice on the full-rank set. With an independent standard Gaussian $k\times q$ matrix $H$, the required conditional law of $F$ is realized by <a id="syn:synthetic-realization"></a>$$\label{syn:synthetic-realization}
 F=HN^{\mathsf T}.$$ The choice of $N$ uses only $B|_L$. The Gaussian restriction $B|_{L^\perp}$ is independent of $B|_L$. Write $s-x=v+w$ with $v\in L$ and $w\in L^\perp$. Then $N^{\mathsf T}Bv=0$, while, conditionally on $B|_L$, the vector $N^{\mathsf T}Bw$ has law $N(0,\lVert w\rVert^2I_q)$. Therefore, conditionally on $H$ and $B|_L$, $$\label{syn:covariance}
 FB(s-x)\sim N(0,\delta(s)^2HH^{\mathsf T}).$$ This conditional calculation accounts for the dependence of $F$ on $B$.

For $\eta>0$, Tonelli’s theorem and <a href="#syn:covariance" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:covariance">[syn:covariance]</a> give $$\mathbb E_{B,F}\int\varphi_{\eta,k}(FB(s-x))\,P(ds)
 =(2\pi)^{-k/2}\int
   \mathbb E_H\det(\delta(s)^2HH^{\mathsf T}+\eta^2I_k)^{-1/2}\,P(ds).$$ The integrand on the right increases as $\eta\downarrow0$. The tube bound <a href="#eq:affine-tube" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:affine-tube">[eq:affine-tube]</a> gives $\delta(s)>0$ for $P$-almost every $s$; it also gives a finite inverse $k$th moment, since $d-1-u>k$. The matrix $H$ has full row rank almost surely. Monotone convergence on the right and Fatou’s lemma on the left, along $\eta=1/j$, prove the first inequality in <a href="#syn:synthetic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:synthetic-bound">[syn:synthetic-bound]</a>.

For completeness, successive orthogonal residuals of the rows of $H$ have independent squared lengths $$V_i\sim\chi^2_{q-i+1},\qquad
 \det(HH^{\mathsf T})=\prod_{i=1}^kV_i.$$ Indeed, conditionally on the preceding rows, the new row is standard Gaussian on a complement of dimension $q-i+1$; this conditional law is constant, which also proves independence of the residual lengths. This is the elementary Gaussian orthogonal reduction described in Edelman and Rao (Edelman and Rao 2005, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[EdelmanRao2005](#ref-EdelmanRao2005)]; the rectangular product used here follows from the displayed conditioning. Direct integration of the chi-squared density gives $\mathbb EV^{-1}=1/(p-2)$ for $V\sim\chi_p^2$, $p>2$. Cauchy–Schwarz hence gives $$a_q=\prod_{i=1}^k\mathbb EV_i^{-1/2}
 \le\prod_{i=1}^k(q-i-1)^{-1/2}
 \le(q-k-1)^{-k/2}.$$

<a id="syn:actual-kernel"></a>
We next use the actual observations. If $Ax=b$ and $L\subset\ker A$, then, for $v=P_L(s-x)$, $$\label{syn:actual-kernel}
 \lVert As-b\rVert=\lVert A(s-x-v)\rVert\le a\,\delta(s).$$ Let $\alpha=d-1-u$. The tube bound, now applied to $x+L$, gives $$P\{\delta\le t\}\le\Lambda C^d t^\alpha,\qquad 0<t\le1.$$ Layer-cake integration therefore yields, for $0<r\le1$, $$\int_{\{\delta\le r\}}\delta^{-k}\,dP
 \le \Lambda C^d
       \left(1+\frac{k}{\alpha-k}\right)r^{\alpha-k}.$$ Since $\alpha-k=d-2k\ge3d/4$, an absolute choice of $C_0$ makes this quantity at most one at $r=r_*$. On $\{\delta>r_*\}$, <a href="#syn:actual-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:actual-kernel">[syn:actual-kernel]</a> gives both $\delta^{-k}\le r_*^{-k}$ and $\delta^{-k}\le a^k\lVert As-b\rVert^{-k}$. The pushforward of $P$ under $A$ has density $g_A$, and both $As$ and $b$ have norm at most $a$. Integration of these two bounds gives <a href="#syn:kernel-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:kernel-bound">[syn:kernel-bound]</a> with the two support cutoffs in <a href="#syn:majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:majorant">[syn:majorant]</a>.

The remaining singularity has the critical exponent $k$ in dimension $k$. Radial integration gives the exact mass <a id="syn:critical-mass"></a>$$\label{syn:critical-mass}
 \int_{\{\lVert w\rVert\le2a\}}
       \min\{(ar_*)^{-k},\lVert w\rVert^{-k}\}\,dw
 =V_k[1+k\log(2/r_*)].$$ The ball of radius $ar_*$ contributes $V_k$, and the annulus contributes $kV_k\int_{ar_*}^{2a}dt/t$. Dropping the outer cutoff in <a href="#syn:majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:majorant">[syn:majorant]</a> only for its convolution term, and using $\int g_A=1$, proves <a href="#syn:majorant-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:majorant-mass">[syn:majorant-mass]</a>.

We record the scale in this bound before taking its logarithm. Integrating the standard Gaussian density over the ball of radius $\sqrt k$ gives $(2\pi)^{-k/2}V_k\le(e/k)^{k/2}$. Also $\mathbb E\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\le C\sqrt d$: choose $1/4$-nets on the two unit spheres of cardinalities at most $9^k$ and $9^d$, bound the operator norm by twice the maximum of the scalar bilinear forms on the nets, and integrate the union bound for their standard Gaussian tails. Jensen’s inequality gives $$\mathbb E\log\bigl[(2\pi)^{-k/2}V_k\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^k\bigr]
 \le\frac k2(1-\log k)+k\log(C\sqrt d)\le Cd.$$ The cutoff <a href="#syn:cutoff" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:cutoff">[syn:cutoff]</a> and <a href="#syn:dimension-margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:dimension-margins">[syn:dimension-margins]</a> give $$\log[2+k\log(2/r_*)]
 \le C+\log d+\log(1+\log\Lambda).$$ These estimates prove <a href="#syn:majorant-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:majorant-log">[syn:majorant-log]</a>. Finally, $\log\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}$ is integrable below because the norm dominates the absolute value of one standard Gaussian entry. Retaining the first term of $U_A$ bounds its total mass below by $(2\pi)^{-k/2}V_k\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}^k$. Together with <a href="#syn:majorant-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:majorant-mass">[syn:majorant-mass]</a>, these observations prove the two remaining integrability assertions. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Integrability and cancellation of the correlation costs

The majorant now controls the synthetic density at the actual label. Before subtracting the two correlation bounds, we verify integrability of the comparison logarithms. This uses both the finite actual correlation and the normalization of $R_B$.

<a id="stmt-syn-logs"></a>
<div id="syn:logs" class="lemma">

<a id="syn:gram-log"></a>
<a id="syn:gram-upper"></a>
<a id="syn:correlation-lower"></a>
**Lemma 34** (Integrability of the projected comparison). *In the replica experiment $Q$, enlarged by the independent $B$ and the conditional $F$ above, $$\mathbb E|\log w(\mathbf Y)|+\mathbb E|\log\Xi|
       +\mathbb E|\log R_B(\mathbf Y)|<\infty.$$ If $G$ is a standard Gaussian $m\times u$ matrix, then $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\log w(\mathbf Y)
 &=\mathbb E\log w(\mathbf X)-\frac{k}{2}\mathbb E\log\det(G^{\mathsf T}G),
 \label{syn:gram-log}\\
 \frac12\mathbb E\log\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)&\le\frac u2\log m.
 \label{syn:gram-upper}
\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\label{syn:correlation-lower}
 \mathcal C_Y
 \ge\mathbb E\log R_B(\mathbf Y)
 \ge\mathbb E\log w(\mathbf Y)-u\,\mathbb E\log\Xi.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-syn-logs"><summary>Proof of stmt-syn-logs</summary>

*Proof.* At a fixed full-affine-rank $\mathbf X$, write $D_{\mathbf X}=UR$, where $U$ has orthonormal columns. Since $B$ is independent, $BU$ is a standard Gaussian $m\times u$ matrix. Consequently $$\Delta(\mathbf Y)\stackrel{\mathrm{law}}{=}
 \Delta(\mathbf X)\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)^{1/2}
 \quad\text{conditionally on }\mathbf X.$$ The logarithm of $\Delta(\mathbf X)$ is integrable by Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a>. Gaussian column residuals factor $\det(G^{\mathsf T}G)$ into independent chi-squared variables of degrees $m,m-1,\ldots,m-u+1$. Their absolute logarithms are integrable from their densities at zero and infinity. This proves <a href="#syn:gram-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:gram-log">[syn:gram-log]</a> and the asserted integrability of $\log w(\mathbf Y)$. Jensen applied to each factor gives <a href="#syn:gram-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:gram-upper">[syn:gram-upper]</a>.

<a id="syn:conditional-mean"></a>
The actual equal-label experiment has $L_{\mathbf X}\subset\ker A$. Conditionally on $(A,b,\mathbf X)$, the matrix $B$ remains independent, and the conditional law of $F$ is $\gamma_{k,m}^{B L_{\mathbf X}}$. Lemma <a href="#syn:synthetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:synthetic">33</a> therefore gives $$\label{syn:conditional-mean}
 \mathbb E[\Xi\mid A,b,\mathbf X]\le a_qU_A(b).$$ It follows that $\mathbb E\log(1+\Xi)<\infty$. Here are the details needed for the possible small values of $g_A(b)$. Conditional Jensen reduces the claim to integrability of $\log(1+U_A(b))$. On the actual finite positive set for $g_A$, $$\log(1+U_A(b))
 \le\log(1+g_A(b))+\log(1+U_A(b)/g_A(b)).$$ The first term is integrable by Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-logs">18</a>. For the second, average over $b$ with density $g_A$ and apply Jensen to obtain at most $\log(1+\int U_A)$. Lemma <a href="#syn:synthetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:synthetic">33</a> makes its average finite. Thus $(\log\Xi)^+$ is integrable.

Let the product reference first draw $B$ and then draw $\ell$ independent points from $P^B$. The divergence of the actual joint law from this reference is $\mathcal C_Y<\infty$, and $R_B$ integrates to one under this reference. Equation <a href="#eq:entropy-positive-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-positive-log">[eq:entropy-positive-log]</a> gives <a id="syn:positive-comparison-log"></a>$$\label{syn:positive-comparison-log}
 \mathbb E(\log R_B(\mathbf Y))^+
 \le\mathbb E\log(1+R_B(\mathbf Y))\le\mathcal C_Y+\log2<\infty.$$ Conditionally on $B,\mathbf Y$, Equation <a href="#syn:comparison-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:comparison-density">[syn:comparison-density]</a> has a finite positive right side, so $\mathbb E_F\Xi^{-u}<\infty$. Hence $\mathbb E_F(\log\Xi)^-<\infty$ on almost every such fiber; also $\mathbb E_F(\log\Xi)^+<\infty$ there by the preceding joint bound. Conditional Jensen is now legitimate and gives $$\log R_B(\mathbf Y)
 \ge\log w(\mathbf Y)-u\,\mathbb E_F\log\Xi.$$ Rearranging this inequality, $$u\,\mathbb E_F(\log\Xi)^-
 \le(\log R_B(\mathbf Y))^+
       +|\log w(\mathbf Y)|+u\,\mathbb E_F(\log\Xi)^+.$$ Its right side has finite expectation, proving joint integrability of $(\log\Xi)^-$. The same Jensen lower bound gives $$(\log R_B(\mathbf Y))^-
 \le|\log w(\mathbf Y)|+u\,\mathbb E_F|\log\Xi|,$$ so the negative comparison logarithm is integrable as well. Finally use the normalized test $R_B$ in <a href="#eq:entropy-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-test">[eq:entropy-test]</a>. All logarithms are now integrable, and that test together with the conditional Jensen bound proves <a href="#syn:correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:correlation-lower">[syn:correlation-lower]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-syn-logs-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-syn-logs</summary>

<a id="syn:likelihood-difference"></a>
<a id="syn:correlation-loss"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#syn:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:block">32</a>.* The preceding lemma permits subtraction of all likelihood and correlation terms. Conditional Jensen in <a href="#syn:conditional-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:conditional-mean">[syn:conditional-mean]</a> gives $$\mathbb E[\log\Xi\mid A,b,\mathbf X]\le\log a_q+\log U_A(b).$$ The last logarithm is integrable under the actual label law: $U_A(b)$ is bounded below there by $(2\pi)^{-k/2}$, and its positive logarithm was controlled in the proof of Lemma <a href="#syn:logs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:logs">34</a>. At fixed $A$, Jensen with $b\sim g_A$ gives $$\int g_A(b)\log\frac{U_A(b)}{g_A(b)}\,db
 \le\log\int U_A(b)\,db.$$ Together with <a href="#syn:synthetic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:synthetic-bound">[syn:synthetic-bound]</a> and <a href="#syn:majorant-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:majorant-log">[syn:majorant-log]</a>, this yields $$\label{syn:likelihood-difference}
 \mathbb E\log\Xi-\mathbb E\log g_A(b)
 \le-\frac k2\log(q-k-1)
       +C[d+\log(1+\log\Lambda)].$$ Combine <a href="#syn:correlation-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:correlation-upper">[syn:correlation-upper]</a>, <a href="#syn:gram-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:gram-log">[syn:gram-log]</a>, <a href="#syn:gram-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:gram-upper">[syn:gram-upper]</a>, <a href="#syn:correlation-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:correlation-lower">[syn:correlation-lower]</a>, and <a href="#syn:likelihood-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:likelihood-difference">[syn:likelihood-difference]</a>. The result is $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal C_X-\mathcal C_Y
 &\le\frac{ku}{2}\log\frac{m}{q-k-1}
       +Cu[d+\log(1+\log\Lambda)] \notag\\
 &\le C\ell[d+\log(1+\log\Lambda)].
 \label{syn:correlation-loss}
\end{aligned}$$ The first term is $O(k u)$ by <a href="#syn:dimension-margins" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:dimension-margins">[syn:dimension-margins]</a>. Thus the scale from the projected Gaussian determinant cancels the inverse-determinant scale in the synthetic likelihood. The critical kernel was integrated before taking a logarithm, leaving only $\log(1+\log\Lambda)$.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:replica-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:replica-information">13</a> with $t=\ell$, the representative $S=X_1$, message $W=z$, and map $\phi_B(x)=Bx$. Every replica has the required signal–message pair law, $B$ is independent of the whole tuple and message, and $\mathcal C_X<\infty$. Its conclusion and $H(z)\le\log K$ give $$\ell I(X_1;z\mid B,BX_1)
 \le\log K+\mathcal C_X-\mathcal C_Y.$$ The joint law of $(B,X_1,z)$ is the product of the $B$-law and the original $(X,z)$-law. Substitution of <a href="#syn:correlation-loss" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:correlation-loss">[syn:correlation-loss]</a> therefore proves <a href="#syn:block-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:block-bound">[syn:block-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Iteration along the full state history

<a id="stmt-syn-streaming"></a>
<div id="syn:streaming" class="proposition">

**Proposition 35** (The synthetic route to the sample bound). *For every nonnegative integer-valued $M(d)=o(d^2)$ and every sequence $0<\epsilon(d)\le1/10$, consider learners in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a> with deterministic horizon $T(d)$ and signal $X\sim\sigma_d$. If their angular success probability, averaged over the uniform signal, rows, and learner randomness, is at least $2/3$, then $$T(d)\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon(d))$$ for all sufficiently large $d$, with an absolute $c>0$. The threshold may depend on the memory sequence.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-syn-streaming"><summary>Proof of stmt-syn-streaming</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> with $p=2/3$ and $p'=s_0=16/25=0.64$ to the learner in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a>. This gives deterministic Borel state-based rules and data-independent initialization, with uniform-prior success at least $s_0$; all remaining randomness is fixed. Pre-generate all $T$ rows, including rows after an early stop.

Put $L_\epsilon=\log(1/\epsilon)$. It suffices to consider dimensions for which $T\le dL_\epsilon$. At other dimensions the claimed lower bound already holds. Lemma <a href="#lem:early-clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:early-clock">11</a>, applied with $p_0=s_0$, gives <a id="syn:output-count"></a>$$\label{syn:output-count}
 s_0\le(T+1)2^M\epsilon^{d-1},\qquad
 L_\epsilon=o(d),\qquad T=o(d^2).$$ Pad first to time $T$ and then to the least multiple of $k$ at least $T$, using the stopping-index tags and ignored fresh pairs of Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a>. Its exact per-index state count gives <a id="syn:padded-width"></a>$$\label{syn:padded-width}
 K=(T+2)2^M,\qquad \log K=o(d^2).$$

<a id="syn:linear-endpoint"></a>
Since $s_0>3/5$, Lemma <a href="#lem:raw-sample-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:raw-sample-bound">12</a> gives $T>\lfloor d/2\rfloor$ for all sufficiently large $d$. We use the weaker consequence $$\label{syn:linear-endpoint}
 T>d/4.$$

<a id="syn:history-information"></a>
Let $p=\lceil T/k\rceil$. Write $H_i$ for the tuple of states at the ends of the first $i$ blocks, with $H_0$ constant. Use one independent $B\sim\gamma_{m,d}$ and $Y=BX$ throughout. If a history $h$ has probability $p_h>0$, Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-priors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-priors">10</a> gives its conditional signal density $$\frac{\mathbb P\{H_{i-1}=h\mid X=x\}}{p_h}\le\frac1{p_h}
 \quad\text{relative to }\sigma_d.$$ The next block matrix is independent of $(X,H_{i-1})$. Given $h$, the initial state is fixed, and the next state is a measurable $K$-valued function of the new block and its exact labels. The side matrix remains independent of this conditional experiment. Proposition <a href="#syn:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="syn:block">32</a> therefore applies with $\Lambda=1/p_h$. Averaging it over histories gives <a id="syn:history-step"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 I(X;H_i\mid H_{i-1},B,Y)
 &\le\frac{\log K}{\ell}
   +C\left[d+\sum_h p_h\log(1+\log(1/p_h))\right] \notag\\
 &\le\frac{\log K}{\ell}
   +C[d+\log(1+(i-1)\log K)].
 \label{syn:history-step}
\end{aligned}$$ The last line uses concavity of $t\mapsto\log(1+t)$ and $H(H_{i-1})\le(i-1)\log K$. In particular it applies the bounded-prior block theorem before averaging its density cost. Since $p=o(d)$, $\ell\ge d/16$, and $\log K=o(d^2)$, the right side is at most $Cd$, with one absolute $C$, for all sufficiently large $d$. The chain rule then gives $$\label{syn:history-information}
 I(X;H_p\mid B,Y)\le Cdp\le C(T+d).$$ The postponed deterministic output is a function of the final tagged state, and therefore of $H_p$.

<a id="syn:history-lower"></a>
To obtain the matching endpoint, use Lemma <a href="#lem:residual-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residual-information">6</a> with $S=X$, $V=H_p$, $G=B$, and $r_0=1/4$. The output is a function of $H_p$, and $B$ is independent of the entire signal–history experiment. Moreover, $2\epsilon<1/4$ and $m\le d/2\le d-2$. For the residual radius $\rho_B=\lVert P_{\ker B}X\rVert$, <a href="#eq:residual-markov" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-markov">[eq:residual-markov]</a> gives $$\mathbb P\{\rho_B<1/4\}
 \le\frac{m/d}{1-1/16}\le\frac8{15}.$$ Thus the lemma applies with success minus radius-failure probability at least $s_0-8/15=8/75$, and yields $$\begin{aligned}
 I(X;H_p\mid B,Y)
 &\ge\frac8{75}(d-m-1)\log\frac1{8\epsilon}-\log2 \notag\\
 &\ge c_1dL_\epsilon
 \label{syn:history-lower}
\end{aligned}$$ for all sufficiently large $d$, with an absolute $c_1>0$. Indeed, $$\log\frac1{8\epsilon}
 \ge\left(1-\frac{\log8}{\log10}\right)L_\epsilon,$$ and $d-m-1\ge d/2-1$.

By <a href="#syn:linear-endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:linear-endpoint">[syn:linear-endpoint]</a>, $T+d<5T$. Combining <a href="#syn:history-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:history-information">[syn:history-information]</a> and <a href="#syn:history-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="syn:history-lower">[syn:history-lower]</a> therefore gives $T\ge c_2dL_\epsilon$ with an absolute $c_2>0$. The constants are absolute; the eventual threshold comes only from $M(d)=o(d^2)$. All replicas and auxiliary projections have served solely to bound information. The learner’s observations remain the prescribed fresh Gaussian rows and exact labels. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:incidence"></a>
### Stiefel incidence and conditional block information

This comparison bounds conditional information directly by the relative entropy of two laws on tuples of signals. Both laws make the signals share an exact projection. The first uses the actual observation block; the second obtains its shared projection from an orthonormal frame inside the coordinates of the independent side projection. Given the separate side projections of all the signals, the second law leaves them conditionally independent with their original fiber laws. This product structure makes the divergence of the two laws an upper bound for the information in the message, multiplied by the number of signals.

The geometric work is to express both laws relative to one incidence measure. We first construct the laws and prove their information comparison. We then compute their exact Jacobians, transfer density exceptions before division, and estimate the resulting likelihood ratio.

<a id="inc:dimensions"></a>
Write $\sigma=\sigma_d$, and let $v_q$ denote the volume of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^q$. For the conditional chain rule on standard Borel spaces, see Austin (Austin 2020, secs. 3.3–3.4) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Austin2020](#ref-Austin2020)]. We use, for all sufficiently large $d$, $$\label{inc:dimensions}
 n=k=\lfloor d/16\rfloor,\qquad
 \ell=k-1,\qquad p=\lfloor d/4\rfloor .$$ Thus $\ell\ge2$, while $p-n-\ell$ and $d-n-\ell$ are at least an absolute positive multiple of $d$.

<a id="stmt-inc-block"></a>
<div id="inc:block" class="proposition">

**Proposition 36** (Information in one block). *Let $\mu=f\sigma$ be a probability measure with a Borel density $0\le f\le F<\infty$, where $F\ge1$. Draw $S\sim\mu$ and standard Gaussian matrices $A\in\mathbb R^{n\times d}$ and $B\in\mathbb R^{p\times d}$, all independently. For an integer $w\ge1$, a message $J$ takes values in a fixed $w$-point alphabet (unused symbols are allowed) and is drawn by an arbitrary measurable probability kernel from $(A,AS)$; conditional on $(A,AS)$, its randomness is independent of $(S,B)$. There is an absolute constant $C$ such that <a id="inc:block-bound"></a>$$\label{inc:block-bound}
 I_\mu(S;J\mid B,BS)
 \le \frac{\log w}{k}+Cd+C\log(1+\log F).$$*

</div>

The dependence on $F$ is doubly logarithmic. This permits us later to condition on an entire finite history: a history of probability $a$ changes the uniform prior to a density bounded by $1/a$.

<a id="inc:laws"></a>
#### Two probability laws on compatible signals

<a id="inc:recalled-fibers"></a>
We use the spherical projection versions from <a href="#eq:finite-tilt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-tilt">[eq:finite-tilt]</a>, with prior $\mu=f\sigma$. Recall them locally: for a full-row-rank $q\times d$ matrix $L$, with $1\le q\le d-2$, and an interior label $t$, let $\sigma_{L,t}$ be uniform probability on the spherical fiber $Ls=t$, and let $p_L^0$ be the uniform-sphere projection density in <a href="#eq:spherical-projection-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spherical-projection-density">[eq:spherical-projection-density]</a>. Then $$\label{inc:recalled-fibers}
 p_L(t)=p_L^0(t)\int f\,d\sigma_{L,t},\qquad
 \mu_{L,t}=\frac{f\,\sigma_{L,t}}{\int f\,d\sigma_{L,t}}
 \quad\text{when }p_L(t)>0.$$ Set $p_L=0$ outside the interior projection ellipsoid and fix one measurable probability kernel on unused fibers. These jointly measurable versions specify all the conditional draws below. Their geometric finite-measure form will enter only when we compute the two likelihoods.

Write $\phi_j(A,y)$ for the probabilities of the message kernel. For a tuple $\mathbf s=(s_1,\ldots,s_k)$, put $$U=[s_2-s_1,\ldots,s_k-s_1]\in\mathbb R^{d\times\ell}.$$ The notation $U^\perp$ means the orthogonal complement of the column space of $U$, and $P_{U^\perp}$ is its orthogonal projector. Let $\mathcal V_{n,h}$ be the manifold of $n\times h$ matrices with orthonormal rows, with the metric induced by the Euclidean matrix norm. Write $\upsilon_{n,h}$ for normalized Riemannian volume on it. This is the Euclidean Stiefel metric discussed by Edelman, Arias, and Smith (Edelman, Arias, and Smith 1998, sec. 2.2.1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[EdelmanAriasSmith1998](#ref-EdelmanAriasSmith1998)]; we compute its volume factor below.

We use an auxiliary $C\in\mathcal V_{n,p}$ and set $D=CB$. For a full-rank $p\times\ell$ matrix $W$, write $\upsilon_W$ for uniform frames whose rows lie in $(\operatorname{col}W)^\perp$. Write $\gamma_{r,d}$ for standard Gaussian measure on $\mathbb R^{r\times d}$, and $\gamma_U$ for the law of $n$ independent $N(0,P_{U^\perp})$ rows. All these kernels are measurable on their rank domains. For example, projecting a Gaussian matrix onto $(\operatorname{col}W)^\perp$ and taking its orthonormal row polar factor gives $\upsilon_W$; orthogonal invariance identifies this with normalized induced volume. We may assign arbitrary values on rank exceptions.

Define $Q$ by the following experiment.

1.  Draw $A\sim\gamma_{n,d}$ and $s_1\sim\mu$ independently. Put $y=As_1$. Draw $J$ from $\phi(A,y)$ and draw $s_2,\ldots,s_k$ independently from $\mu_{A,y}$, independently of $J$ conditional on $(A,y)$.

2.  Draw $B\sim\gamma_{p,d}$ independently. Given $(\mathbf s,B)$, draw $C\sim\upsilon_{BU}$.

<a id="inc:q-marginals"></a>
Then $AU=0$. Conditional on $(A,y)$, all $k$ signals are independent with law $\mu_{A,y}$, which is absolutely continuous on a positive-radius sphere of dimension $d-n-1$. Each new point therefore avoids the affine span of its predecessors; hence $U$ has rank $\ell$ almost surely. The independent Gaussian $B$ makes $BU$ have rank $\ell$ almost surely, so $\upsilon_{BU}$ is available because $p-\ell\ge n$. Each marginal $$\label{inc:q-marginals}
 \mathcal L_Q(s_i,J,B,Bs_i)=\mathcal L_\mu(S,J,B,BS)
 \qquad(1\le i\le k)$$ is the law in Proposition <a href="#inc:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:block">36</a>.

<a id="inc:conditional-D"></a>
The matrix $D=CB$ has a useful conditional law under $Q$: $$\label{inc:conditional-D}
 \mathcal L_Q(D\mid \mathbf s,A,y,J)
 =N(0,P_{U^\perp})^{\otimes n}.$$ To verify this, split each row of $B$ between $\operatorname{col}U$ and $U^\perp$. The second component is independent of $BU$. Given the tuple, the kernel for $C$ depends on $B$ only through $BU$; its orthonormal rows send the independent Gaussian second component to $n$ independent standard Gaussian rows in $U^\perp$. The first component is annihilated by $C$.

Define $R$ in the other order. First draw independent $B\sim\gamma_{p,d}$ and $C\sim\upsilon_{n,p}$; then $D=CB$ is standard Gaussian. Draw $s_1\sim\mu$ independently and, conditional on $(D,Ds_1)$, draw $s_2,\ldots,s_k$ independently from $\mu_{D,Ds_1}$. Draw $J$ uniformly from its $w$ possible values, independently, and finally draw $A\sim\gamma_U$. Again $U$ has rank $\ell$. Conditional on $(C,D)$, the other $p-n$ rows in an orthogonal row rotation of $B$ are independent standard Gaussian rows and were not used to draw the tuple. Since $p-n\ge\ell$, they make $BU$ have rank $\ell$ almost surely. In these experiments $A$ and $D$ also have row rank $n$ almost surely: each is either standard Gaussian or has conditional Gaussian support $U^\perp$, of dimension $d-\ell\ge n$.

<a id="stmt-inc-product"></a>
<div id="inc:product" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:product-law"></a>
<a id="inc:entropy-lower"></a>
**Lemma 37** (Product conditioning). *Put $Z_i=Bs_i$. After $A,C$ are discarded under $R$, $$\label{inc:product-law}
 \mathcal L_R(\mathbf s\mid B,Z_1,\ldots,Z_k,J)
 =\bigotimes_{i=1}^k\mu_{B,Z_i}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{inc:entropy-lower}
 \operatorname{KL}(Q\|R)\ge kI_\mu(S;J\mid B,BS).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-product"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-product</summary>

*Proof.* Retain $B,C$ temporarily and put $t=Ds_1$. Conditional on $(B,C,t)$, the signals are independent with law $\mu_{D,t}$. Disintegrating this law further according to $z=Bs$ gives $\mu_{B,z}$: the earlier equation $Ds=t$ is just $Cz=t$, already determined by $z$. Formally, disintegrate the law of $z=Bs$ according to $Cz$, and compose its conditional kernel with $\mu_{B,z}$; the resulting kernel is a conditional law given $Ds=t$. These identities hold at the needed values because each individual signal has marginal law $\mu$ given $(B,C)$. Conditioning the independent signals on their separate $Z_i$’s therefore yields the product in <a href="#inc:product-law" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:product-law">[inc:product-law]</a>. That product does not depend on $C,t$, so discarding them leaves it unchanged. The independent uniform $J$ and the later kernel for $A$ do not change the marginal under consideration.

Data processing and the conditional chain rule give $$\operatorname{KL}(Q\|R)\ge
 \mathbb E_Q\operatorname{KL}\!\left(
 Q(\mathbf s\mid B,Z_1,\ldots,Z_k,J)
 \,\middle\|\,\bigotimes_{i=1}^k\mu_{B,Z_i}\right).$$ This is valid as an extended inequality; absolute continuity will be established below. Relative entropy to a product is at least the sum of the relative entropies of the marginals. For each such summand, convexity of relative entropy permits us, after averaging, to discard the other $Z_j$’s from its first conditional law. The result is at least $$\mathbb E_Q\operatorname{KL}\bigl(
 Q(s_i\mid B,Z_i,J)\,\|\,\mu_{B,Z_i}\bigr)
 =I_\mu(S;J\mid B,BS)$$ by <a href="#inc:q-marginals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:q-marginals">[inc:q-marginals]</a>. Sum over $i$. ◻

</details>

The product conditional law has reduced the block information bound to one task: bound $\operatorname{KL}(Q\|R)$ by $\log w$ plus the geometric and prior-density costs. We next put both laws on a common incidence measure. Its exact densities will make that divergence computable, after exceptional sets have been removed before division.

<a id="inc:common-measure"></a>
#### The common measure and its density versions

<span id="inc:fibers" label="inc:fibers"></span>

To compare the two laws, we express their conditional probabilities as normalized finite measures on the same incidence set. The underlying geometric formula is the regular-submersion case of coarea; see Federer (Federer 1959, Definition 2.10 and Theorem 3.1, pp. 423 and 426–427) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Federer1959](#ref-Federer1959)]. The following calculation specifies the induced fiber volume and the probability normalization used here.

<a id="stmt-inc-projection"></a>
<div id="inc:projection" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:fiber-measure"></a>
<a id="inc:ellipsoid-bound"></a>
**Lemma 38** (Spherical projection measure). *Suppose $L\in\mathbb R^{q\times d}$ has full row rank and $1\le q\le d-2$. In the interior $$E_L=\{t:t^{\mathsf T}(LL^{\mathsf T})^{-1}t<1\}$$ define a finite measure on $\{s\in\mathbb S^{d-1}:Ls=t\}$ by $$\label{inc:fiber-measure}
 \lambda_{L,t}(ds)=
 \frac{f(s)}{|\mathbb S^{d-1}|\,j_L(s)}
 \,d\operatorname{vol}_{d-1-q}(s),\qquad
 j_L(s)=\det\!\bigl(L(I-ss^{\mathsf T})L^{\mathsf T}\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ Set $\lambda_{L,t}=0$ outside $E_L$. Its mass and normalization are exactly the versions recalled in <a href="#inc:recalled-fibers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:recalled-fibers">[inc:recalled-fibers]</a>: $$p_L(t)=\lambda_{L,t}(\mathbb S^{d-1}),\qquad
 \mu_{L,t}=\lambda_{L,t}/p_L(t)\quad\text{where }p_L(t)>0.$$ The finite-measure kernel is jointly measurable in $(L,t)$. Moreover, $$\label{inc:ellipsoid-bound}
 V_L\|p_L\|_\infty\le Fd^d,\qquad
 V_L=|E_L|=v_q\det(LL^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-projection"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-projection</summary>

*Proof.* The tangent space at $s$ is $s^\perp$. The Gram matrix of the differential $L:s^\perp\to\mathbb R^q$ is $L(I-ss^{\mathsf T})L^{\mathsf T}$, proving the formula for its normal Jacobian. With $P_L=L^{\mathsf T}(LL^{\mathsf T})^{-1}L$, the determinant lemma also gives $$j_L(s)=\det(LL^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}\sqrt{1-\|P_Ls\|^2}.$$ The differential is onto except when $s$ belongs to the row space of $L$; that sphere has zero $(d-1)$-dimensional surface measure. Applying change of variables on coordinate patches of its complement, and then exhausting the complement, gives $$\int h(s)\,d\mu(s)
 =\int_{\mathbb R^q}\int h(s)\,\lambda_{L,t}(ds)\,dt
 \quad(h\ge0).$$ To identify these measures with the versions already used, put $s_0=L^{\mathsf T}(LL^{\mathsf T})^{-1}t$ and $r=\sqrt{1-\|s_0\|^2}$. An interior fiber is $s_0+r\mathbb S(\ker L)$, where $\mathbb S(\ker L)$ is the unit sphere of $\ker L$, and $j_L$ is constant there. Its induced volume is $|\mathbb S^{d-q-1}|r^{d-q-1}$. Dividing this volume by $|\mathbb S^{d-1}|j_L$ gives precisely the uniform projection density $p_L^0(t)$ in <a href="#eq:spherical-projection-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:spherical-projection-density">[eq:spherical-projection-density]</a>. Consequently, as finite measures on every interior fiber, $$\lambda_{L,t}=p_L^0(t)f\,\sigma_{L,t}.$$ Taking mass and then normalizing gives <a href="#inc:recalled-fibers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:recalled-fibers">[inc:recalled-fibers]</a> pointwise on its positive set. A uniform direction in $\ker L$ can be generated by projecting a standard Gaussian vector onto that subspace and normalizing. The projection matrix is a measurable function of full-rank $L$; integrating the bounded Borel function $f(s_0+r\theta)$ against this kernel therefore gives measurable $\lambda_{L,t}$, $p_L(t)$, and $\mu_{L,t}$, with the fixed convention on unused fibers from <a href="#inc:recalled-fibers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:recalled-fibers">[inc:recalled-fibers]</a>.

For orthonormal rows and $f=1$, the displayed fiber formula gives a density proportional to $(1-\|t\|^2)^{(d-q-2)/2}$ on the unit ball. Its normalizing integral is at least $\tfrac12v_qd^{-q/2}$: restrict to the ball of radius $d^{-1/2}$ and use $(1-1/d)^{(d-q-2)/2}\ge1/2$. The maximum of the density times $v_q$ is consequently at most $2d^{q/2}\le d^d$. The bound $f\le F$ multiplies this estimate by at most $F$. Finally, an invertible change from orthonormal row coordinates to $L$ divides the density by $\det(LL^{\mathsf T})^{1/2}$ and multiplies the ellipsoid volume by the same factor. This proves <a href="#inc:ellipsoid-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:ellipsoid-bound">[inc:ellipsoid-bound]</a>; in particular our chosen $p_L$ is finite everywhere. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-inc-swap"></a>
<div id="inc:swap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 39** (Interchange on a regular incidence set). *Let $X,Z$ be second-countable Riemannian manifolds, and let $G:X\times Z\to\mathbb R^a$ be smooth. On a relatively open subset $\Omega$ of $G^{-1}(0)$, suppose both partial differentials $d_xG$ and $d_zG$ are onto. Write $J_xG,J_zG$ for their normal Jacobians. For every nonnegative measurable $h$ on $\Omega$, <a id="inc:swap-identity"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_Z\int_{\{x:(x,z)\in\Omega\}}
       \frac{h(x,z)}{J_xG(x,z)}
       \,d\operatorname{vol}_{\dim X-a}(x)\,d\operatorname{vol}_Z(z)
       \notag\\
 &\qquad=
 \int_X\int_{\{z:(x,z)\in\Omega\}}
       \frac{h(x,z)}{J_zG(x,z)}
       \,d\operatorname{vol}_{\dim Z-a}(z)\,d\operatorname{vol}_X(x).
 \label{inc:swap-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ The fiber volumes are induced volumes, and the equality holds for nonnegative integrals even when their common value is infinite.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-swap"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-swap</summary>

*Proof.* Near a point of $\Omega$, choose coordinates $x=(x',x'')$, $z=(z',z'')$ so that $x''$ and $z''$ each have $a$ coordinates and both $G_{x''}$ and $G_{z''}$ are invertible. Let $u_X(x)\,dx$, $u_Z(z)\,dz$ be the volume forms in these coordinates. Changing variables from $(x',x'')$ to $(x',G)$ shows that the induced $x$-fiber volume divided by $J_xG$ is $$\frac{u_X(x)}{|\det G_{x''}|}\,dx'.$$ Indeed, the tangent-volume factor and the normal-volume factor of this change of variables are respectively the induced fiber volume and the reciprocal normal Jacobian. Thus the left integral has local density $u_Xu_Z/|\det G_{x''}|$ in $(x',z',z'')$; the right integral has density $u_Xu_Z/|\det G_{z''}|$ in $(x',z',x'')$. At fixed $(x',z')$, implicit differentiation gives $$\frac{dx''}{dz''}=-G_{x''}^{-1}G_{z''},\qquad
 \left|\det\frac{dx''}{dz''}\right|
 =\frac{|\det G_{z''}|}{|\det G_{x''}|}.$$ The two local integrals agree. Split $\Omega$ into measurable pieces subordinate to a countable coordinate cover and sum using Tonelli’s theorem. This also proves the assertion for infinite integrals. ◻

</details>

Let $\mathsf H$ be the probability law that draws $\mathbf s\sim\mu^{\otimes k}$, then independent $B\sim\gamma_{p,d}$, and, conditional on these, independently draws $A\sim\gamma_U$ and $C\sim\upsilon_{BU}$. Include an independent uniform $J$. Independent points from $\mu$ give $\operatorname{rank}U=\ell$ almost surely. The same Gaussian decomposition as in <a href="#inc:conditional-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:conditional-D">[inc:conditional-D]</a> then gives full ranks for $BU,A,D$ under $\mathsf H$.

<a id="stmt-inc-densities"></a>
<div id="inc:densities" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:undivided-A"></a>
<a id="inc:undivided-C"></a>
**Lemma 40** (Exact undivided identities and probability densities). *Define the following marginal probability measures of $\mathsf H$: $$\begin{split}
 \mathsf H_A(d\mathbf s,dA)
   &=\mu^{\otimes k}(d\mathbf s)\,\gamma_U(dA),\\
 \mathsf H_C(d\mathbf s,dB,dC)
   &=\mu^{\otimes k}(d\mathbf s)\,\gamma_{p,d}(dB)\,\upsilon_{BU}(dC).
 \end{split}$$ Using the unnormalized measures of <a href="#inc:fiber-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:fiber-measure">[inc:fiber-measure]</a>, define nonnegative measures on the corresponding exact incidence sets by <a id="inc:MA"></a><a id="inc:MC"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathsf M_A(d\mathbf s,dA)
  &=\mu(ds_1)\gamma_{n,d}(dA)
       \prod_{i=2}^k\lambda_{A,As_1}(ds_i),\label{inc:MA}\\
 \mathsf M_C(d\mathbf s,dB,dC)
  &=\mu(ds_1)\gamma_{p,d}(dB)\upsilon_{n,p}(dC)
       \prod_{i=2}^k\lambda_{D,Ds_1}(ds_i),\qquad D=CB.\label{inc:MC}
\end{aligned}$$ Values at row-rank failures may be set to zero. The weights on the right below are assigned arbitrary finite values on their Gram-rank null sets. These measures satisfy the exact identities $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathsf M_A
  &=(2\pi)^{-n\ell/2}\det(U^{\mathsf T}U)^{-n/2}\,\mathsf H_A,
       \label{inc:undivided-A}\\
 \mathsf M_C
  &=\kappa\det(U^{\mathsf T}B^{\mathsf T}BU)^{-n/2}\,\mathsf H_C,
 \qquad
 \kappa=\frac{|\mathcal V_{n,p-\ell}|}{|\mathcal V_{n,p}|}.
       \label{inc:undivided-C}
\end{aligned}$$ These are equalities of nonnegative measures.*

<a id="inc:q-density"></a>
<a id="inc:r-density"></a>
*For $L=A,D$, the values $p_L(Ls_1)$ are positive and finite $\mathsf H$-almost surely and equal the shrinking-ball limits $$p_L(Ls_1)=\lim_{j\to\infty}
 \frac{\mu\{z:\|Lz-Ls_1\|<2^{-j}\}}{v_n2^{-jn}}.$$ On this full-measure set, the densities of $Q$ and $R$ with respect to $\mathsf H$ are $$\begin{aligned}
 q_*&=w\phi_J(A,As_1)(2\pi)^{-n\ell/2}
       \det(U^{\mathsf T}U)^{-n/2}p_A(As_1)^{-\ell},
       \label{inc:q-density}\\
 r_*&=\kappa\det(U^{\mathsf T}B^{\mathsf T}BU)^{-n/2}
       p_D(Ds_1)^{-\ell}.\label{inc:r-density}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular $Q\ll R$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-densities"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-densities</summary>

<a id="inc:MA-marginal"></a>
<a id="inc:MC-marginal"></a>
*Proof.* We first establish the identities without dividing by any projection density. In $\mathsf M_A$, integrating the tuple variables gives the exact $(s_1,A)$-marginal $$\label{inc:MA-marginal}
 \mu(ds_1)\gamma_{n,d}(dA)\,p_A(As_1)^\ell.$$ For fixed full-rank $A$, this weight is finite by <a href="#inc:ellipsoid-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:ellipsoid-bound">[inc:ellipsoid-bound]</a>. Where it is positive, $\lambda_{A,As_1}^{\otimes\ell}
=p_A(As_1)^\ell\mu_{A,As_1}^{\otimes\ell}$. The rank argument for the $Q$ experiment therefore shows that $\operatorname{rank}U=\ell$ outside an $\mathsf M_A$-null set. At zero weight the fiber product has zero mass. The same reasoning in $\mathsf M_C$ gives the exact $(s_1,B,C)$-marginal $$\label{inc:MC-marginal}
 \mu(ds_1)\gamma_{p,d}(dB)\upsilon_{n,p}(dC)\,p_D(Ds_1)^\ell,$$ and the rank argument for $R$ removes the loci where $U$ or $BU$ has rank less than $\ell$. Multiplication by the finite weight in <a href="#inc:MC-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:MC-marginal">[inc:MC-marginal]</a> preserves these native null sets.

On either incidence set, the spherical partial differentials are regular on the remaining locus. Indeed, if some $s_i$ belonged to the row space of $A$, then its row-space projection would have norm one. The equations $As_j=As_i$ would give every $s_j$ that same row-space projection, and their unit norms would force $s_j=s_i$ for all $j$, contradicting $\operatorname{rank}U=\ell$. The same argument applies to $D$. The Gaussian rank assertions above also remove row-rank failures. Thus removing the complements of the full-rank regular loci changes neither $\mathsf M_A,\mathsf M_C$ nor the corresponding $\mathsf H_A,\mathsf H_C$.

Fix $s_1$ and apply Lemma <a href="#inc:swap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:swap">39</a> to $$x=(s_2,\ldots,s_k),\qquad z=A,\qquad G(x,z)=AU.$$ For fixed $A$, the target has $\ell$ independent $n$-coordinate blocks, so the partial normal Jacobian is $\prod_{i=2}^k j_A(s_i)$. These are precisely the denominators in $\prod_{i=2}^k\lambda_{A,As_1}(ds_i)$. For fixed tuple, the differential is $\Delta A\mapsto\Delta A\,U$. It consists of $n$ row maps with Gram matrix $U^{\mathsf T}U$; its normal Jacobian is $\det(U^{\mathsf T}U)^{n/2}$. The $A$-fiber has rows in $U^\perp$. Restricting the ambient standard Gaussian density to this subspace and then expressing it relative to the normalized Gaussian $\gamma_U$ contributes $(2\pi)^{-n\ell/2}$. This proves <a href="#inc:undivided-A" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:undivided-A">[inc:undivided-A]</a> on the regular locus, and hence exactly after the null loci just proved are removed. In particular, integrating over a restricted regular tuple fiber gives at most $p_A(As_1)^\ell$, and gives equality for native almost every $(s_1,A)$.

For the second identity, fix $(s_1,B)$ and use $G(x,C)=CBU$. Put $W=BU$. The tuple partial normal Jacobian is $\prod_{i=2}^k j_D(s_i)$. At $CW=0$, $$T_C\mathcal V_{n,p}
 =\{\Delta:\Delta C^{\mathsf T}+C\Delta^{\mathsf T}=0\}.$$ Variations whose rows belong to $\operatorname{col}W$ lie in this tangent space, since $C W=0$. They form the orthogonal complement of the tangent space to the constrained frame fiber. On this normal space the differential is $\Delta\mapsto\Delta W$, with normal Jacobian $\det(W^{\mathsf T}W)^{n/2}$. There is no additional metric factor in these normal directions. The constrained fiber, with its induced metric, is isometric to $\mathcal V_{n,p-\ell}$. Replacing its unnormalized volume by $\upsilon_W$ leaves the volume ratio $\kappa$. Lemma <a href="#inc:swap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:swap">39</a> now proves <a href="#inc:undivided-C" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:undivided-C">[inc:undivided-C]</a>, again exactly after removal of native rank-null sets. The corresponding restricted tuple mass is at most $p_D(Ds_1)^\ell$ and equals it for native almost every $(s_1,B,C)$.

We next transfer the density exceptions using these undivided measures. For a full-rank $n\times d$ matrix $L$, define $$b_j(L,t)=
 \frac{\mu\{z:\|Lz-t\|<2^{-j}\}}{v_n2^{-jn}},$$ and let $\mathcal E(L,s)$ be the condition that either $p_L(Ls)\notin(0,\infty)$, or the limit of $b_j(L,Ls)$ does not exist, or that limit differs from $p_L(Ls)$. This is a measurable condition. Set $$\mathcal E_A=\{(\mathbf s,A):\mathcal E(A,s_1)\},\qquad
 \mathcal E_D=\{(\mathbf s,B,C):\mathcal E(CB,s_1)\}.$$ For every fixed full-rank $L$, Lebesgue differentiation and the density formula imply $\mu\{s:\mathcal E(L,s)\}=0$. The exact native marginals <a href="#inc:MA-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:MA-marginal">[inc:MA-marginal]</a> and <a href="#inc:MC-marginal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:MC-marginal">[inc:MC-marginal]</a>, whose density weights are finite for each fixed native projection, therefore give $$\mathsf M_A(\mathcal E_A)=0,\qquad
 \mathsf M_C(\mathcal E_D)=0.$$ On the right sides of <a href="#inc:undivided-A" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:undivided-A">[inc:undivided-A]</a> and <a href="#inc:undivided-C" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:undivided-C">[inc:undivided-C]</a>, the multiplying Gram weights are strictly positive and finite almost surely. If $h$ denotes either weight, then on $\{1/m\le h\le m\}$ the equality $\int_{\mathcal E}h\,d\mathsf H_\bullet=0$ gives $\mathsf H_\bullet(\mathcal E\cap\{1/m\le h\le m\})=0$. Letting $m\to\infty$ proves that $\mathcal E_A$ is $\mathsf H_A$-null and $\mathcal E_D$ is $\mathsf H_C$-null. This argument is valid even if an undivided measure has infinite total mass. Adding the unused probability kernels gives both assertions under $\mathsf H$.

Only now replace each unnormalized fiber measure by its normalized conditional law. The $Q$ experiment divides <a href="#inc:undivided-A" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:undivided-A">[inc:undivided-A]</a> by $p_A(As_1)^\ell$, then attaches the same kernels for $B,C$ and the message density $w\phi_J(A,As_1)$ relative to uniform $J$. This gives <a href="#inc:q-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:q-density">[inc:q-density]</a>. The $R$ experiment divides <a href="#inc:undivided-C" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:undivided-C">[inc:undivided-C]</a> by $p_D(Ds_1)^\ell$ and attaches the unchanged $\gamma_U$ kernel for $A$ and uniform $J$, giving <a href="#inc:r-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:r-density">[inc:r-density]</a>. The divisions are legitimate on the full-measure set just proved. Since $r_*>0$ and is finite there, $Q\ll R$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-inc-stiefel"></a>
<div id="inc:stiefel" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:stiefel-volume"></a>
<a id="inc:kappa"></a>
**Lemma 41** (The induced Stiefel volume). *For $1\le n\le h$, with the induced Euclidean matrix metric, $$\label{inc:stiefel-volume}
 |\mathcal V_{n,h}|=
 2^{n(n-1)/4}\prod_{j=1}^n|\mathbb S^{h-j}|.$$ Consequently, for <a href="#inc:dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:dimensions">[inc:dimensions]</a>, $$\label{inc:kappa}
 \kappa=\pi^{-n\ell/2}\prod_{j=1}^n
 \frac{\Gamma((p-j+1)/2)}{\Gamma((p-j+1-\ell)/2)}
 \ge\left(\frac{p-n+1-\ell}{2\pi}\right)^{n\ell/2}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-stiefel"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-stiefel</summary>

*Proof.* For $n=1$, the frame manifold is $\mathbb S^{h-1}$, giving the base case of <a href="#inc:stiefel-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:stiefel-volume">[inc:stiefel-volume]</a>. For $n\ge2$, the map from $\mathcal V_{n,h}$ to its first row in $\mathbb S^{h-1}$ has constant normal Jacobian $2^{-(n-1)/2}$. To compute it, move the frame by an orthogonal transformation to the first $n$ coordinate rows. Tangent matrices then consist of a skew-symmetric block in the first $n$ columns and an arbitrary remaining block. Each of the $n-1$ skew coordinates affecting the first row appears twice in the squared matrix norm, so the corresponding singular value of the first-row map is $2^{-1/2}$. The other first-row coordinates have singular value one. The fiber is isometric to $\mathcal V_{n-1,h-1}$. Slicing thus gives $$|\mathcal V_{n,h}|=
 2^{(n-1)/2}|\mathbb S^{h-1}|\,|\mathcal V_{n-1,h-1}|.$$ Iteration proves <a href="#inc:stiefel-volume" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:stiefel-volume">[inc:stiefel-volume]</a>. The metric factor depends only on $n$, so it cancels in the ratio defining $\kappa$. The sphere-area formula $|\mathbb S^{h-1}|=2\pi^{h/2}/\Gamma(h/2)$ gives the product in <a href="#inc:kappa" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:kappa">[inc:kappa]</a>.

For $a>0$ and $\ell/2\ge1$, a gamma random variable $X$ of shape $a$ and scale one satisfies $\Gamma(a+\ell/2)/\Gamma(a)=\mathbb E X^{\ell/2}
\ge(\mathbb E X)^{\ell/2}=a^{\ell/2}$ by Jensen’s inequality. Apply this with $a=(p-j+1-\ell)/2$ and then use $a\ge(p-n+1-\ell)/2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="inc:inverse-section"></a>
#### An inverse kernel at two scales

The density ratio in Lemma <a href="#inc:densities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:densities">40</a> involves $p_D(Ds_1)/p_A(As_1)$. It has no useful pointwise bound. We control its positive logarithm instead, using the fact that $A$ annihilates the same difference space that governs the conditional Gaussian law of $D$. For $u>0$, write $\log^+u=\max\{0,\log u\}$, and set $\log^+0=0$.

<a id="stmt-inc-kernel"></a>
<div id="inc:kernel" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:kernel-bound"></a>
<a id="inc:conditional-density"></a>
**Lemma 42** (Two-scale inverse kernel). *Under $Q$, define $$\begin{split}
 K&=\int
   \operatorname{dist}(z-s_1,\operatorname{col}U)^{-n}\,d\mu(z),\\
 p_A^*(t)&=\sup_{\rho>0}
   \frac{\mu\{z:\|Az-t\|<\rho\}}{v_n\rho^n},\qquad
 K_0=v_n\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^n p_A^*(As_1).
 \end{split}$$ Then $K_0\ge2^{-n}$, and $$\label{inc:kernel-bound}
 K\le K_0L_F,\qquad
 L_F=1+\frac{n}{d-\ell-n}+n\log(2/\tau),\qquad
 \tau=\left(\frac{2^{-n}}{Fd^d}\right)^{1/(d-\ell-n)}.$$ In particular, $\log L_F\le C\log d+C\log(1+\log F)$, and $K<\infty$. Moreover, $$\label{inc:conditional-density}
 \mathbb E_Q[p_D(Ds_1)\mid\mathbf s,A,y,J]
 \le (2\pi)^{-n/2}K .$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-kernel"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-kernel</summary>

*Proof.* For fixed $z$, <a href="#inc:conditional-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:conditional-D">[inc:conditional-D]</a> makes the coordinates of $D(z-s_1)$ independent centered Gaussians with variance $\operatorname{dist}(z-s_1,\operatorname{col}U)^2$. Hence $$\frac{\Pr_Q\{\|D(z-s_1)\|<\rho\mid\mathbf s,A,y,J\}}{v_n\rho^n}
 \le (2\pi)^{-n/2}
       \operatorname{dist}(z-s_1,\operatorname{col}U)^{-n}.$$ The right side is interpreted as $+\infty$ when the distance is zero. Integrate in $z$ using Tonelli’s theorem and then apply conditional Fatou along $\rho=2^{-j}$. Lemma <a href="#inc:densities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:densities">40</a> and $Q\ll\mathsf H$ identify the almost-sure limit of the ball averages with $p_D(Ds_1)$. This proves <a href="#inc:conditional-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:conditional-density">[inc:conditional-density]</a> without evaluating an arbitrary density version at a dependent point.

Let $H(t)$ be the $\mu$-probability that the distance in $K$ is at most $t$. Since $AU=0$, $$\operatorname{dist}(z-s_1,\operatorname{col}U)\le t
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \|A(z-s_1)\|\le\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}t.$$ The projection law has a Lebesgue density, so projection-ball boundaries have zero mass. The definition of $p_A^*$ therefore gives $H(t)\le K_0t^n$. All these distances are at most two; taking $t\downarrow2$ from above also gives $K_0\ge2^{-n}$.

<a id="inc:two-scales"></a>
A second estimate uses the projection onto $U^\perp$, of dimension $d-\ell\le d-2$. Applying Lemma <a href="#inc:projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:projection">38</a> to a matrix with orthonormal rows for this projection bounds its density by $Fd^d/v_{d-\ell}$. A ball of radius $t$ centered at the projection of $s_1$ consequently has probability at most $Fd^dt^{d-\ell}$. Thus $$\label{inc:two-scales}
 H(t)\le\min\{K_0t^n,\;Fd^dt^{d-\ell}\}.$$ The exponent $d-\ell$ uses the bounded projection density and the condition $\ell\ge2$.

The layer-cake formula, including the possibility of an initially infinite integral, is $$K=2^{-n}+n\int_0^2H(t)t^{-n-1}\,dt.$$ Here $d-\ell-n>0$ and $\tau\le1$. Use the second estimate in <a href="#inc:two-scales" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:two-scales">[inc:two-scales]</a> up to $\tau$ and the first thereafter: $$\begin{split}
 K&\le2^{-n}
   +\frac{nFd^d\tau^{d-\ell-n}}{d-\ell-n}
   +nK_0\log(2/\tau)\\
 &\le K_0\left(1+\frac{n}{d-\ell-n}+n\log(2/\tau)\right).
 \end{split}$$ The last inequality uses $Fd^d\tau^{d-\ell-n}=2^{-n}\le K_0$. Finally, $$\log(1/\tau)
 =\frac{n\log2+\log F+d\log d}{d-\ell-n}.$$ The dimension ratios in <a href="#inc:dimensions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:dimensions">[inc:dimensions]</a> give $L_F\le C(1+d\log d+\log F)$. Taking its logarithm gives the stated bound and finiteness of the inverse-distance integral $K$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-inc-maximal"></a>
<div id="inc:maximal" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:maximal-bound"></a>
**Lemma 43** (Maximal average at an actual label). *For every full-rank $A$, $$\label{inc:maximal-bound}
 \int\log\frac{p_A^*(As)}{p_A(As)}\,d\mu(s)
 \le Cd+C\log(1+\log F).$$ The integrand is nonnegative almost surely.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-maximal"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-maximal</summary>

*Proof.* Lebesgue differentiation gives $p_A^*(t)\ge p_A(t)$ for $A_\#\mu$-almost every label. We prove the particular centered maximal inequality we need. Its one-dimensional ancestry is the maximal-function theory of Hardy and Littlewood (Hardy and Littlewood 1930, sec. III) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[HardyLittlewood1930](#ref-HardyLittlewood1930)]; for the dimension cost of customary covering arguments, see Stein and Strömberg (Stein and Strömberg 1983, 259) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[SteinStromberg1983](#ref-SteinStromberg1983)].

<a id="inc:weak-maximal"></a>
For $a>0$, a compact subset of $\{t:p_A^*(t)>a\}$ has a finite cover by balls centered in that set, each of projection mass greater than $a$ times its volume. Select disjoint balls in decreasing order of radius. Every discarded ball is contained in the triple of a selected ball, since it meets a selected ball of at least its radius. The selected masses sum to at most one, so the compact set has volume at most $3^n/a$. Inner regularity gives $$\label{inc:weak-maximal}
 |\{t:p_A^*(t)>a\}|\le3^n/a\qquad(a>0).$$ The function is measurable: the supremum may be restricted to positive rational radii, because a density gives zero mass to ball boundaries.

Let $E_A$ be the projection ellipsoid and $V_A=|E_A|$. Since $p_A^*\le\|p_A\|_\infty$, integration of level sets, using the bound $V_A$ up to level $1/V_A$ and <a href="#inc:weak-maximal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:weak-maximal">[inc:weak-maximal]</a> above that level, gives $$\int_{E_A}p_A^*(t)\,dt
 \le1+3^n\log^+(V_A\|p_A\|_\infty)
 \le1+3^n(\log F+d\log d).$$ Jensen’s inequality with respect to the probability density $p_A$ then yields $$\int p_A(t)\log\frac{p_A^*(t)}{p_A(t)}\,dt
 \le\log\int_{\{p_A>0\}}p_A^*(t)\,dt
 \le\log\int_{E_A}p_A^*(t)\,dt.$$ Values on $\{p_A=0\}$ do not enter the integral. The preceding estimate and $n\asymp d$ prove <a href="#inc:maximal-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:maximal-bound">[inc:maximal-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-maximal-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-maximal</summary>

<a id="inc:gram-term"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#inc:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:block">36</a>.* On the full-measure set in Lemma <a href="#inc:densities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:densities">40</a>, the log likelihood ratio is the sum of the message term $\log(w\phi_J(A,As_1))$, the term $$\label{inc:gram-term}
 -\frac{n\ell}{2}\log(2\pi)-\log\kappa+
 \frac n2\log
 \frac{\det(U^{\mathsf T}B^{\mathsf T}BU)}{\det(U^{\mathsf T}U)},$$ and $\ell\log(p_D(Ds_1)/p_A(As_1))$. The message term is at most $\log w$.

Under $Q$, $B$ is independent of $U$. Conditional on $U$, put $V=U(U^{\mathsf T}U)^{-1/2}$. Then $G=BV$ is a standard Gaussian $p\times\ell$ matrix and $$\frac{\det(U^{\mathsf T}B^{\mathsf T}BU)}{\det(U^{\mathsf T}U)}
 =\det(G^{\mathsf T}G).$$ The lower bound in <a href="#inc:kappa" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:kappa">[inc:kappa]</a> and the determinant–trace inequality bound <a href="#inc:gram-term" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:gram-term">[inc:gram-term]</a> above by $$\frac{n\ell}{2}\log
 \frac{\operatorname{tr}(G^{\mathsf T}G)}
      {\ell(p-n+1-\ell)}.$$ Its positive part has expectation $O(n\ell)$. Indeed, $\mathbb E\operatorname{tr}(G^{\mathsf T}G)=p\ell$, the ratio $p/(p-n+1-\ell)$ is bounded, and $\mathbb E\log^+X\le\log(1+\mathbb E X)$ for $X\ge0$.
