<a id="inc:log-ratio-conditional"></a>
For the remaining ratio, use $\log^+u\le\log(1+u)$, conditional Jensen, and <a href="#inc:conditional-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:conditional-density">[inc:conditional-density]</a> and <a href="#inc:kernel-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:kernel-bound">[inc:kernel-bound]</a>. Since $p_A^*(As_1)/p_A(As_1)\ge1$ almost surely, this gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\mathbb E_Q\!\left[
   \log^+\frac{p_D(Ds_1)}{p_A(As_1)}
   \,\middle|\,\mathbf s,A,y,J\right]\notag\\
 &\quad\le \log2+
  \log^+\!\bigl((2\pi)^{-n/2}v_n\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^n\bigr)
  +\log L_F+\log\frac{p_A^*(As_1)}{p_A(As_1)}.
 \label{inc:log-ratio-conditional}
\end{aligned}$$ The Gaussian operator term has expectation $O(n)$. Here are the elementary estimates. A $1/4$-net of the unit sphere in $\mathbb R^h$ has size at most $9^h$, by packing disjoint radius $1/8$ balls. Nets in the two dimensions bound $\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}$ by twice the maximum of at most $9^{n+d}$ absolute standard Gaussian bilinear forms. A Gaussian tail bound gives $\mathbb E\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}\le C\sqrt d$. Also, integrating the Gaussian density over the ball of radius $\sqrt n$ gives $v_n\le(2\pi e/n)^{n/2}$. Therefore $$\log^+\!\bigl((2\pi)^{-n/2}v_n\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^n\bigr)
 \le n\log(1+\sqrt{e/n}\,\|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}),$$ whose expectation is $O(n)$ by Jensen and $n\asymp d$.

The $(A,s_1)$-marginal under $Q$ is the original independent Gaussian/prior draw. Averaging <a href="#inc:log-ratio-conditional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:log-ratio-conditional">[inc:log-ratio-conditional]</a> and using Lemmas <a href="#inc:kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:kernel">42</a> and <a href="#inc:maximal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:maximal">43</a> consequently gives <a id="inc:log-ratio"></a>$$\label{inc:log-ratio}
 \mathbb E_Q\log^+\frac{p_D(Ds_1)}{p_A(As_1)}
 \le Cd+C\log(1+\log F).$$ All positive parts in the log likelihood ratio are now integrable. Since $Q\ll R$, its negative part is integrable as well: with $h=dQ/dR$, $\int_{\{h<1\}}-h\log h\,dR\le1/e$. Thus no undefined subtraction occurs, and $$\operatorname{KL}(Q\|R)
 \le\log w+Ckd+Ck\log(1+\log F).$$ Combine this with <a href="#inc:entropy-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:entropy-lower">[inc:entropy-lower]</a> and divide by $k$. ◻

</details>

<a id="inc:history-section"></a>
#### Finite histories and the precision endpoint

We state the consequence first for average success under the uniform spherical prior, using the exact finite-state randomized model in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a>.

<a id="stmt-inc-precision"></a>
<div id="inc:precision" class="theorem">

<a id="inc:precision-bound"></a>
**Theorem 44** (Average-prior precision bound). *There is a universal $c>0$ with the following property. Let $M=M(d)$ be a nonnegative integer with $M(d)=o(d^2)$. For all sufficiently large $d$, with the threshold allowed to depend on the rate $M(d)/d^2\to0$, suppose $S\sim\sigma$ and a learner in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a> stops by a deterministic integer horizon $T\ge0$. If $$\Pr\{\angle(\widehat S,S)\le\epsilon\}\ge2/3,
 \qquad 0<\epsilon\le1/10,$$ then $$\label{inc:precision-bound}
 T\ge c\,d\log(1/\epsilon).$$ The bound permits arbitrary computation within a transition.*

</div>

For the proof of Theorem <a href="#inc:precision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:precision">44</a> and its auxiliary lemmas below, apply the fixed-shared-seed part of Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a> to the stated learner in Definition <a href="#def:streaming-model" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:streaming-model">3</a>. We work in the resulting Borel experiment with data-independent initialization and uniform-prior success at least $2/3$, retaining fresh transition and output randomness. This is the standing reduction for this subsection.

<a id="stmt-inc-short"></a>
<div id="inc:short" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:width"></a>
<a id="inc:short-bounds"></a>
**Lemma 45** (Short-horizon reduction). *Put $L_\epsilon=\log(1/\epsilon)$. Pad stopping to $T$ by retaining the stopped state and its stopping index. At most $$\label{inc:width}
 w=(T+2)2^M$$ padded states suffice. Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#inc:precision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:precision">44</a>, if $T<dL_\epsilon$, then $$\label{inc:short-bounds}
 L_\epsilon=O(1+M/d)=o(d),\qquad
 T=O(d^2),\qquad \log w=o(d^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-short"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-short</summary>

*Proof.* The padding count in Lemma <a href="#lem:padding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:padding">9</a>, including stopping at index zero, gives <a href="#inc:width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:width">[inc:width]</a>. Its stopped records remain fixed while fresh unused observations are generated. The output-capacity bound in Lemma <a href="#lem:early-clock" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:early-clock">11</a>, with success at least $2/3$ and $(T+1)2^M\le w$, gives $$\frac23\le w\epsilon^{d-1},\qquad
 (d-1)L_\epsilon\le
 M\log2+\log(T+2)+\log(3/2).$$ Because $L_\epsilon\ge\log10$ and $T<dL_\epsilon$, $\log(T+2)\le\log d+\log L_\epsilon+C$. Using $\log L_\epsilon\le L_\epsilon$ yields $$(d-2)L_\epsilon\le M\log2+\log d+C.$$ This proves $L_\epsilon=O(1+M/d)=o(d)$. The remaining assertions follow from $T<dL_\epsilon$ and $M=o(d^2)$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-inc-history"></a>
<div id="inc:history" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:history-bound"></a>
**Lemma 46** (Information accumulated over histories). *In the short-horizon case of Lemma <a href="#inc:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:short">45</a>, put $b=\lceil T/n\rceil$ and pad with fresh unused samples to $bn$ observations. Let $V_i$ be the transcript of the first $i$ block-end padded states, with $V_0$ trivial. Let $B\sim\gamma_{p,d}$ be independent of the entire learner experiment and used only for analysis. Then, for all sufficiently large $d$, $$\label{inc:history-bound}
 I(S;V_b\mid B,BS)\le Cbd$$ for an absolute $C$. The dimension threshold may depend on the rate $M/d^2\to0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-history"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-history</summary>

*Proof.* For a positive-probability history $V_{i-1}=v$, Lemma <a href="#lem:conditional-priors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:conditional-priors">10</a> gives the Bayes density $$f_v(s)=\frac{\Pr\{V_{i-1}=v\mid S=s\}}{\Pr\{V_{i-1}=v\}}
 \le F_v:=\frac1{\Pr\{V_{i-1}=v\}}$$ relative to $\sigma$. A Borel version is available from the Borel learner. The history fixes the previous padded state. Its update over the next fresh block is a kernel of $(A,AS)$ to at most $w$ next states. For the first block, independent initialization can be integrated into the same kernel. The matrix $B$ is absent from the learner and independent of its entire experiment; it remains independent of the entire conditional signal, fresh-block, and message experiment after conditioning on $v$. Proposition <a href="#inc:block" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:block">36</a> therefore applies at each such history.

The finite history has entropy $H(V_{i-1})\le b\log w$. Hence Jensen’s inequality gives $$\mathbb E_v\log(1+\log F_v)
 \le\log(1+H(V_{i-1}))
 \le\log(1+b\log w)=O(\log d).$$ The last bound uses $b=O(d)$ and $\log w=O(d^2)$ eventually, from <a href="#inc:short-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:short-bounds">[inc:short-bounds]</a> and $n\asymp d$. The same bounds give $\log w/k=o(d)$. Averaging the one-block estimate thus bounds each increment $I(S;V_i\mid V_{i-1},B,BS)$ by $Cd$ eventually. The conditional chain rule, with the same independent $B$ throughout, proves <a href="#inc:history-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:history-bound">[inc:history-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-inc-fiber-precision"></a>
<div id="inc:fiber-precision" class="lemma">

<a id="inc:fiber-bound"></a>
**Lemma 47** (Precision inside an independent fiber). *Let $S\sim\sigma$, and let a unit-vector output $\widehat S$ have angular error at most $\epsilon\le1/10$ with probability at least $2/3$. Let $B\sim\gamma_{p,d}$, $p=\lfloor d/4\rfloor$, be independent of the entire experiment producing $(S,\widehat S)$. Then, for all sufficiently large $d$, $$\label{inc:fiber-bound}
 I(S;\widehat S\mid B,BS)
 \ge \frac{d-p-1}{3}\log\frac1{4\epsilon}-\log2
 \ge c_1dL_\epsilon$$ with an absolute $c_1>0$. In the learner model above, success probability at least $2/3$ is also impossible for $T\le d/4$, even if all observations are retained.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-fiber-precision"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-fiber-precision</summary>

*Proof.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:residual-information" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residual-information">6</a> with $V=\widehat S$, the identity output kernel, $b=p$, and $r_0=1/2$. For the residual radius $R=\|P_{\ker B}S\|$, Lemma <a href="#lem:residual-sphere" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:residual-sphere">5</a> gives $$\Pr\{R<1/2\}\le\frac{4p}{3d}\le\frac13.$$ Success at least $2/3$ therefore leaves event mass at least $1/3$, and <a href="#eq:residual-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:residual-information">[eq:residual-information]</a> gives the first inequality in <a href="#inc:fiber-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:fiber-bound">[inc:fiber-bound]</a>. The second follows from $$\log\frac1{4\epsilon}
 \ge\left(1-\frac{\log4}{\log10}\right)L_\epsilon,$$ because $d-p-1$ is an absolute positive fraction of $d$; the subtractive $\log2$ is absorbed for large $d$.

For the final assertion, grant the learner all pre-generated observations through its horizon, including ignored rows after stopping. Its output is a kernel of those data and independent randomness. Lemma <a href="#lem:raw-sample-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:raw-sample-bound">12</a> shows that success at least $3/5$, and hence at least $2/3$, already requires $T>\lfloor d/2\rfloor$ for large $d$. In particular, $T>d/4$. That lemma includes the deterministic zero-row residual sphere, so the assertion covers $T=0$ as well. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-inc-fiber-precision-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-inc-fiber-precision</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#inc:precision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:precision">44</a>.* If $T\ge dL_\epsilon$, the desired bound is immediate. Otherwise apply Lemmas <a href="#inc:short" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:short">45</a> and <a href="#inc:history" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:history">46</a>. The terminal output is a kernel of the final padded state, hence of $V_b$; conditional data processing and Lemma <a href="#inc:fiber-precision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:fiber-precision">47</a> give $$Cbd\ge I(S;V_b\mid B,BS)
 \ge I(S;\widehat S\mid B,BS)
 \ge c_1dL_\epsilon.$$ Thus $b\ge c_0L_\epsilon$ for an absolute $c_0>0$. If $L_\epsilon\ge2/c_0$, then $$T>n(b-1)\ge(c_0/2)nL_\epsilon\ge c\,dL_\epsilon$$ after adjusting an absolute constant. If $L_\epsilon<2/c_0$, the separate conclusion $T>d/4$ of Lemma <a href="#inc:fiber-precision" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="inc:fiber-precision">47</a> gives the same order after another absolute adjustment. This proves <a href="#inc:precision-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="inc:precision-bound">[inc:precision-bound]</a> uniformly over the allowed $\epsilon$. ◻

</details>

A guarantee of success probability at least $2/3$ for every fixed unit signal implies the uniform-prior average guarantee, so it has the same consequence. The argument uses fresh exact labels, a finite persistent state alphabet, and a deterministic horizon; its additional projections and tuple resampling belong only to the analysis.
